Typed Non-determinism in Functional and Concurrent Calculi
Bas van den Heuvel, Joseph W. N. Paulus, Daniele Nantes-Sobrinho, Jorge A. Pérez

Abstract
We study functional and concurrent calculi with non-determinism, along with type systems to control resources based on linearity. The interplay between non-determinism and linearity is delicate: careless handling of branches can discard resources meant to be used exactly once. Here we go beyond prior work by considering non-determinism in its standard sense: once a branch is selected, the rest are discarded. Our technical contributions are three-fold. First, we introduce a π \pi -calculus with non-deterministic choice, governed by session types. Second, we introduce a resource λ \lambda -calculus, governed by intersection types, in which non-determinism concerns fetching of resources from bags. Finally, we connect our two typed non-deterministic calculi via a correct translation.



1
Introduction

In this paper, we present new formulations of typed programming calculi with
non-determinism
.
A classical ingredient of models of computation, non-determinism brings flexibility and generality in specifications.
In process calculi such as CCS and the
\pi
-calculus, one source of non-determinism is choice, which is typically
non-confluent
: that is, given
P+Q
, we have either
P+Q\longrightarrow P
or
P+Q\longrightarrow Q
.
Thus, committing to a branch entails discarding the rest.

We study non-determinism as a way of increasing the expressivity of typed calculi in which resource control is based on
linearity
.
The interplay between non-determinism and linearity is delicate: a careless discarding of branches can jeopardize resources meant to be used exactly once.
On the concurrent side, we consider the
\pi
-calculus,
the paradigmatic model of concurrency
[
26
]
.
We focus on
\pi
-calculi with
session types

[
13
,
14
]
, in which linear logic principles ensure communication correctness:
here the resources are names that perform session protocols; they can be
unrestricted
(used multiple times) and
linear
(used exactly once).
To properly control resources, non-confluent non-determinism is confined to unrestricted names; linear names can only perform deterministic choices.

In this context, considering
confluent
forms of non-determinism can be appealing.
Intuitively, such formulations allow all branches to proceed independently: given
P_{1}\longrightarrow Q_{1}
and
P_{2}\longrightarrow Q_{2}
, then
P_{1}+P_{2}\longrightarrow Q_{1}+P_{2}

and

P_{1}+P_{2}\longrightarrow P_{1}+Q_{2}
.
Because confluent non-determinism does not discard branches, it is compatible with a resource-conscious view of computation.

Confluent non-determinism has been studied mostly in the functional setting; it is present, e.g., in
Pagani and Ronchi della Rocca’s resource
\lambda
-calculus
[
19
]

and in
Ehrhard and Regnier’s differential
\lambda
-calculus
[
10
]
.
In
[
19
]
, non-determinism resides in the application of a term
M
to a
bag
of available resources
C
; a
\beta
-reduction applies
M
to a resource
non-deterministically fetched
from
C
.
Confluent non-deterministic choice is also present in the session-typed
\pi
-calculus by Caires and Pérez
[
5
]
, where it expresses a choice between different implementations of the same session protocols, which are all
non-deterministically available
—they may be available but may also
fail
.
In their work, a Curry-Howard correspondence between linear logic and session types (‘
propositions-as-sessions
’
[
6
,
30
]
) ensures confluence, protocol fidelity, and deadlock-freedom.
Paulus et al.
[
20
]
relate functional and concurrent calculi with confluent non-determinism: they give a translation
of a resource
\lambda
-calculus into the session
\pi
-calculus from
[
5
]
, in the style of Milner’s ‘
functions-as-processes
’
[
16
]
.

Although results involving confluent non-determinism are most significant, usual (non-confluent) non-determinism remains of undiscussed convenience in formal modeling; consider, e.g., specifications of distributed protocols
[
2
,
18
]
in which commitment is essential.
Indeed, non-confluent non-deterministic choice is commonplace in verification frameworks such as mCRL2
[
12
]
.
It is also relevant in functional calculi; a well-known framework is De’Liguoro and Piperno’s (untyped) non-deterministic

\lambda
-calculus
[
8
]
(see also
[
9
]
and references therein).

To further illustrate the difference between confluent and non-confluent non-determinism, we consider an example adapted from
[
5
]
: a movie server that offers a choice between buying a movie or watching its trailer.
In
{{\mathsf{s}\pi\mkern-2.0mu}^{+}\mkern-1.0mu}
, the typed
\pi
-calculus that we present in this paper, this server can be specified as follows:

\mathsf{Server}_{s}=s.\mathtt{case}\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\mathsf{buy}:s(\textit{title});s(\textit{paym});\overline{s}[\texttt{movie}];\overline{s}[]~,\\
\mathsf{peek}:s(\textit{title});\overline{s}[\texttt{trailer}];\overline{s}[]\end{array}\right\}\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}-\mathsf{Server}_{s}^{\mathsf{buy}}\\
-\mathsf{Server}_{s}^{\mathsf{peek}}\end{array}

where
s(-)
and
\overline{s}[-]
denote input and output prefixes on a name/channel
s
, respectively, and ‘
movie
’ and ‘
trailer
’ denote references to primitive data.
Also, the free names of a process are denoted with subscripts.
Process
\mathsf{Server}_{s}
offers a choice on name
s
(
s.\mathtt{case}\{-\}
) between labels
buy
and
peek
.
If
buy
is received, process
\mathsf{Server}_{s}^{\mathsf{buy}}
is launched: it receives the movie’s title and a payment method, sends the movie, and closes the session on
s
(
\overline{s}[]
).
If
peek
is received, it proceeds as
\mathsf{Server}_{s}^{\mathsf{peek}}
: the server receives the title, sends the trailer, and closes the session.

Using the non-deterministic choice operator of
{{\mathsf{s}\pi\mkern-2.0mu}^{+}\mkern-1.0mu}
, denoted ‘
\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}
’, we can specify a process for a client Alice who is interested in the movie ‘Jaws’ but is undecided about buying the film or just watching its trailer for free:

\mathsf{Alice}_{s}:=\begin{array}[t]{@{}r@{}lll@{}}&\overline{s}.\mathsf{buy};&\overline{s}[\texttt{Jaws}];\overline{s}[\texttt{mcard}];s(\textit{movie});s();\bm{0}&-\mathsf{Alice}_{s}^{\mathsf{buy}}\\
{}\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}&\overline{s}.\mathsf{peek};&\overline{s}[\texttt{Jaws}];s(\textit{trailer});s();\bm{0}&-\mathsf{Alice}_{s}^{\mathsf{peek}}\end{array}

If
\mathsf{Alice}_{s}
selects the label
\mathsf{buy}
(
\overline{s}.\mathsf{buy}
), process
\mathsf{Alice}_{s}^{\mathsf{buy}}
is launched: it sends title and payment method, receives the movie, waits for the session to close (
s()
), and then terminates (
\bm{0}
).
If
\mathsf{Alice}_{s}
selects
peek
, process
\mathsf{Alice}_{s}^{\mathsf{peek}}
is launched: it sends a title, receives the trailer, waits for the session to close, and terminates. Then, process
\mathsf{Sys}:=(\bm{\nu}s)(\mathsf{Server}_{s}\mathbin{|}\mathsf{Alice}_{s})
denotes the composition of client and server, connected along
s
(using
(\bm{\nu}s)
).
Our semantics for
{{\mathsf{s}\pi\mkern-2.0mu}^{+}\mkern-1.0mu}
, denoted
\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.38,0.61,0.27}\leadsto}}
, enforces non-confluent non-determinism, as
\mathsf{Sys}
can reduce to separate processes, as expected:

\mathsf{Sys}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.38,0.61,0.27}\leadsto}}(\bm{\nu}s)(\mathsf{Server}_{s}^{\mathsf{buy}}\mathbin{|}\mathsf{Alice}_{s}^{\mathsf{buy}})\text{and}\mathsf{Sys}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.38,0.61,0.27}\leadsto}}(\bm{\nu}s)(\mathsf{Server}_{s}^{\mathsf{peek}}\mathbin{|}\mathsf{Alice}_{s}^{\mathsf{peek}})

In contrast, the confluent non-deterministic choice from
[
5
]
, denoted ‘
\oplus
’, behaves differently: in their confluent semantics,
\mathsf{Sys}
reduces to a
single
process including
both alternatives
, i.e.,
\mathsf{Sys}\mathbin{\longrightarrow}(\bm{\nu}s)(\mathsf{Server}_{s}^{\mathsf{buy}}\mathbin{|}\mathsf{Alice}_{s}^{\mathsf{buy}})\oplus(\bm{\nu}s)(\mathsf{Server}_{s}^{\mathsf{peek}}\mathbin{|}\mathsf{Alice}_{s}^{\mathsf{peek}})
.

Contributions.

We study new concurrent and functional calculi with usual (non-confluent) forms of non-determinism.
Framed in the typed (resource-conscious) setting,
we strive for definitions that do not exert a too drastic discarding of branches (as in the non-confluent case) but also that do not exhibit a lack of commitment (as in the confluent case).
Concretely, we present:

(
\lx@sectionsign

2
)

{{\mathsf{s}\pi\mkern-2.0mu}^{+}\mkern-1.0mu}
, a variant of the session-typed
\pi
-calculus in
[
5
]
, now with non-confluent non-deterministic choice.
Its semantics adapts to the typed setting the usual semantics of non-deterministic choice in the untyped
\pi
-calculus
[
26
]
.
Well-typed processes enjoy type preservation and deadlock-freedom (
Theorems

2.1
and
2.2
).

(
\lx@sectionsign

3
)

\lambda_{\mathtt{C}}^{\ell}
, a resource
\lambda
-calculus with non-determinism, enhanced with constructs for expressing resource usage and failure.
Its non-idempotent intersection type system provides a quantitative measure of the need/usage of resources.
Well-typed terms enjoy subject reduction and subject expansion (
Theorems

3.1
and
3.2
).

(
\lx@sectionsign

4
)
A typed translation of
\lambda_{\mathtt{C}}^{\ell}
into
{{\mathsf{s}\pi\mkern-2.0mu}^{+}\mkern-1.0mu}
, which
provides further validation for our non-deterministic calculi, and casts them in the context of ‘functions-as-processes’.
We prove that our translation is
correct
, i.e., it preserves types and satisfies tight operational correspondences (
Theorems

4.1
and
4.2
).

Moreover,
\lx@sectionsign

5
closes by discussing related works.
Due to space limitations,
appendices contain (i) omitted material (in particular, proofs of technical results); (ii) an alternative
eager
semantics for
{{\mathsf{s}\pi\mkern-2.0mu}^{+}\mkern-1.0mu}
, which we compare against the lazy semantics;
and (iii) extensions of
{{\mathsf{s}\pi\mkern-2.0mu}^{+}\mkern-1.0mu}
and
\lambda_{\mathtt{C}}^{\ell}
with
unrestricted
resources.

2
A Typed
\pi
-calculus with Non-deterministic Choice

We introduce
{{\mathsf{s}\pi\mkern-2.0mu}^{+}\mkern-1.0mu}
, a session-typed
\pi
-calculus with non-deterministic choice.
Following
[
5
]
, session types express protocols to be executed along channels.
These protocols can be
non-deterministic
: sessions may succeed but also fail.
The novelty in
{{\mathsf{s}\pi\mkern-2.0mu}^{+}\mkern-1.0mu}
is the non-deterministic choice operator ‘
\!P\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}Q
’, whose
lazily committing semantics
is
compatible with linearity.
We prove that well-typed processes satisfy two key properties:
type preservation
and
deadlock-freedom
.

\displaystyle P,Q

\displaystyle::=\bm{0}

inaction

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}[x\mathbin{\leftrightarrow}y]

forwarder

\displaystyle~~~\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}(\bm{\nu}x)(P\mathbin{|}Q)

connect

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}P\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}Q

non-determinism

\displaystyle~~~\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}\overline{x}[y];(P\mathbin{|}Q)

output

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}x(y);P

input

\displaystyle~~~\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}\overline{x}.\ell;P

select

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}x.\mathtt{case}\{i:P\}_{i\in I}

branch

\displaystyle~~~\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}\overline{x}[]

close

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}x();P

wait

\displaystyle~~~\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}{x.\mathtt{some}}_{w_{1},\ldots,w_{n}};P

expect

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}\overline{x}.\mathtt{some};P

available

\displaystyle~~~\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}P\mathbin{|}Q

parallel

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}\overline{x}.\mathtt{none}

unavailable

.

\displaystyle\equiv P^{\prime}~[P\equiv_{\alpha}P^{\prime}]

\displaystyle[x\mathbin{\leftrightarrow}y]

\displaystyle\equiv[y\mathbin{\leftrightarrow}x]

\displaystyle P\mathbin{|}\bm{0}

\displaystyle\equiv P

\displaystyle(P\mathbin{|}Q)\mathbin{|}R

\displaystyle\equiv P\mathbin{|}(Q\mathbin{|}R)

\displaystyle P\mathbin{|}Q

\displaystyle\equiv Q\mathbin{|}P

\displaystyle(\bm{\nu}x)(P\mathbin{|}Q)

\displaystyle\equiv(\bm{\nu}x)(Q\mathbin{|}P)

\displaystyle(\bm{\nu}x)((\bm{\nu}y)(P\mathbin{|}Q)\mathbin{|}R)

\displaystyle[x\notin\mathit{fn}(Q),y\notin\mathit{fn}(R)]

Figure 1:

{{\mathsf{s}\pi\mkern-2.0mu}^{+}\mkern-1.0mu}
: syntax (top) and structural congruence (bottom).

2.1
Syntax and Semantics

We use
P,Q,\ldots
to denote processes, and
x,y,z,\ldots
to denote
names
representing channels.
Figure

1
(top) gives the syntax of processes.

P\{y/z\}
denotes the capture-avoiding substitution of
y
for
z
in
P
.
Process
\bm{0}
denotes inaction, and

[x\mathbin{\leftrightarrow}y]
is a forwarder: a bidirectional link between
x
and
y
.
Parallel composition appears in two forms:
while the process
P\mathbin{|}Q
denotes communication-free concurrency,
process
(\bm{\nu}x)(P\mathbin{|}Q)
uses restriction
(\bm{\nu}x)
to express that
P
and
Q
implement complementary behaviors on
x
and do not share any other names.

Process
P\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}Q
denotes the non-deterministic choice between
P
and
Q
: intuitively, if one choice can perform a synchronization, the other option may be discarded if it cannot.
Since
\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}
is associative, we often omit parentheses. Also, we write
\scalebox{1.5}{$\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}\mkern-3.0mu$}_{i\in I}P_{i}
for the non-deterministic choice between each
P_{i}
for
i\in I
.

Our output construct integrates parallel composition and restriction: process
\overline{x}[y];(P\mathbin{|}Q)
sends a fresh name
y
along
x
and then continues as
P\mathbin{|}Q
.
The type system will ensure that behaviors on
y
and
x
are implemented by
P
and
Q
, respectively, which do not share any names—this separation defines communication-free concurrency and is key to ensuring deadlock-freedom.
The input process
x(y);P
receives a name
z
along
x
and continues as
P\{z/y\}
, which does not require the separation present in the output case.
Process
x.\mathtt{case}\{i:P_{i}\}_{i\in I}
denotes a branch with labeled choices indexed by the finite set
I
: it awaits a choice on
x
with continuation
P_{j}
for each
j\in I
.
The process
\overline{x}.\ell;P
selects on
x
the choice labeled
\ell
before continuing as
P
.
Processes
\overline{x}[]
and
x();P
are dual actions for closing the session on
x
.
We omit replicated servers
{!}x(y);P
and corresponding client requests
{?}\overline{x}[y];P
, but they can be easily added (cf.
\lx@sectionsign

0.A
).

The remaining constructs define non-deterministic sessions which
may provide a protocol or fail, following
[
5
]
.
Process
\overline{x}.\mathtt{some};P
confirms the availability of a session on
x
and continues as
P
.
Process
\overline{x}.\mathtt{none}
signals the failure to provide the session on
x
.
Process
{x.\mathtt{some}}_{w_{1},\ldots,w_{n}};P
specifies a dependency on a non-deterministic session on
x
(names
w_{1},\ldots,w_{n}
implement sessions in
P
). This
process can either (i) synchronize with a ‘
\overline{x}.\mathtt{some}
’ and continue as
P
, or (ii) synchronize
with a ‘
\overline{x}.\mathtt{none}
’, discard
P
, and propagate the failure to
w_{1},\ldots,w_{n}
.
To reduce eye strain, in writing
{x.\mathtt{some}}
we freely combine names and sets of names.
This way, e.g.,
we write
{x.\mathtt{some}}_{y,\mathit{fn}(P),\mathit{fn}(Q)}
rather than
{x.\mathtt{some}}_{\{y\}\cup\mathit{fn}(P)\cup\mathit{fn}(Q)}
.

Name
y
is bound in
(\bm{\nu}y)(P\mathbin{|}Q)
,
\overline{x}[y];(P\mathbin{|}Q)
, and
x(y);P
.
We write
\mathit{fn}(P)
and
\mathit{bn}(P)
for the free and bound names of
P
, respectively. We adopt Barendregt’s convention.

Structural Congruence.

Reduction defines the steps that a process performs on its own.
It relies on
structural congruence
(
\equiv
), the least congruence relation on processes induced by the rules in
Figure

1
(bottom).
Like the syntax of processes, the definition of
\equiv
is aligned with the type system (defined next), such that
\equiv
preserves typing (subject congruence, cf.
Theorem

2.1
).
Differently from
[
5
]
, we do not allow distributing non-deterministic choice over parallel and restriction.
As shown in
\lx@sectionsign

0.B
, the position of a non-deterministic choice in a process determines how it may commit, so changing its position affects commitment.

Reduction: Intuitions and Prerequisites.

Barring non-deterministic choice, our reduction rules arise as directed interpretations of proof transformations in the underlying linear logic.
We follow Caires and Pfenning
[
6
]
and Wadler
[
30
]
in interpreting cut-elimination in linear logic as synchronization in
{{\mathsf{s}\pi\mkern-2.0mu}^{+}\mkern-1.0mu}
.

Before delving into our reduction rules (
Figure

2
), it may be helpful to consider the usual reduction axiom for the (untyped)
\pi
-calculus (e.g.,
[
17
,
26
]
):

(\overline{x}[z];P_{1}+M_{1})\mathbin{|}(x(y);P_{2}+M_{2})\longrightarrow P_{1}\mathbin{|}P_{2}\{z/y\}\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:usualpi}

(1)

This axiom captures the interaction of two (binary) choices: it integrates the commitment of choice in synchronization; after the reduction step, the two branches not involved in the synchronization,
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
, are discarded.
Our semantics of
{{\mathsf{s}\pi\mkern-2.0mu}^{+}\mkern-1.0mu}
is defined similarly: when a prefix within a branch of a choice synchronizes with its dual, that branch reduces and the entire process commits to it.

The key question at this point is: when and to which branches should we commit?
In (
1
), a communication commits to a single branch.
For
{{\mathsf{s}\pi\mkern-2.0mu}^{+}\mkern-1.0mu}
, we define a
lazy semantics
that minimizes commitment as much as possible.

The intuitive idea is that multiple branches of a choice may contain the same prefix, and so all these branches represent possibilities for synchronization (“possible branches”).
Other branches with different prefixes denote different possibilities (“impossible branches”).
When one synchronization is chosen, the possible branches are maintained while the impossible ones are discarded.

Example 1

To distinguish possible and impossible branches, consider:

\displaystyle P:=(\bm{\nu}s)\mkern-1.0mu\big(\mkern-2.0mus.\mathtt{case}\{\mathsf{buy}:\mathinner{\ldotp\kern-1.99997pt\ldotp\kern-1.99997pt\ldotp},\mathsf{peek}:\mathinner{\ldotp\kern-1.99997pt\ldotp\kern-1.99997pt\ldotp}\}\mathbin{|}(\overline{s}.\mathsf{buy};\mathinner{\ldotp\kern-1.99997pt\ldotp\kern-1.99997pt\ldotp}\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}\overline{s}.\mathsf{buy};\mathinner{\ldotp\kern-1.99997pt\ldotp\kern-1.99997pt\ldotp}\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}\overline{s}.\mathsf{peek};\mathinner{\ldotp\kern-1.99997pt\ldotp\kern-1.99997pt\ldotp})\mkern-3.0mu\big)

The branch construct (case) provides the context for the non-deterministic choice.
When the case synchronizes on the ‘
buy
’ label, the two branches prefixed by ‘
\overline{s}.\mathsf{buy}
’ are possible, whereas the branch prefixed by ‘
\overline{s}.\mathsf{peek}
’ becomes impossible, and can be discarded. The converse occurs when the ‘
peek
’ label is selected.

∎

To formalize these intuitions, our reduction semantics (
Figure

2
) relies on some auxiliary definitions. First, we define contexts.

Definition 1
.

We define
ND-contexts
(
\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{N}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{M}}
) as follows:

{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{N}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{M}}::=[\cdot]\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{N}}\mathbin{|}P\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}(\bm{\nu}x)({\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{N}}\mathbin{|}P)\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{N}}\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}P

The process obtained by replacing
[\cdot]
in
{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{N}}
with
P
is denoted
{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{N}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}[}P{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}]}
.
We refer to ND-contexts that do not use the clause ‘
{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{N}}\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}P
’ as
D-contexts
, denoted
{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{C}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{D}}
.

Using D-contexts, we can express that, e.g.,
\scalebox{1.5}{$\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}\mkern-3.0mu$}_{i\in I}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{C_{i}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}[}\overline{x}[]{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}]}
and
\scalebox{1.5}{$\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}\mkern-3.0mu$}_{j\in J}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{D_{j}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}[}x();Q_{j}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}]}
should match.
To account for reductions with impossible branches, we define a precongruence on processes, denoted
\mathrel{\succeq_{S}}
, where the parameter
S
denotes the subject(s) of the prefix in the possible branches.
Our semantics is closed under
\mathrel{\succeq_{S}}
.
Hence, e.g., anticipating a reduction on
x
, the possible branch
{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{C_{1}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}[}x(y);P{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}]}
can be extended with an impossible branch to form
{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{C_{1}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}[}x(y);P{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}]}\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{C_{2}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}[}z();Q{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}]}
.

Before defining
\mathrel{\succeq_{S}}
(
Definition

3
), we first define prefixes (and their subjects).
Below, we write
\widetilde{x}
to denote a finite tuple of names
x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}
.

Definition 2
.

Prefixes are defined as follows:

\alpha,\beta::=\overline{x}[y]\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}x(y)\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}\overline{x}.\ell\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}x.\mathtt{case}\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}\overline{x}[]\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}x()\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}\overline{x}.\mathtt{some}\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}\overline{x}.\mathtt{none}\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}{x.\mathtt{some}}_{\widetilde{w}}\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}[x\mathbin{\leftrightarrow}y]

The subjects of
\alpha
, denoted
\mathit{subj}\{\alpha\}
, are
\{x,y\}
in case of
[x\mathbin{\leftrightarrow}y]
, or
\{x\}
.
By abuse of notation, we write
\alpha;P
even when
\alpha
takes no continuation (as in
\overline{x}[]
,
\overline{x}.\mathtt{none}
, and
[x\mathbin{\leftrightarrow}y]
) and for
\overline{x}[y]
which takes a parallel composition as continuation.

Definition 3
.

Let
\bowtie
denote the least relation on prefixes (Def.
2
) defined by:

(i)
\overline{x}[y]\bowtie\overline{x}[z]
, (ii)
x(y)\bowtie x(z)
, and (iii)
\alpha\bowtie\alpha
otherwise.

Given a non-empty set
S\subseteq\{x,y\}
, the precongruence
P\mathrel{\succeq_{S}}Q
holds when both following conditions hold:

1.

S=\{x\}

implies

P=\Big(\scalebox{1.5}{$\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}\mkern-3.0mu$}_{i\in I}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{C_{i}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}[}\alpha_{i};P_{i}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}]}\Big)\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}\Big(\scalebox{1.5}{$\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}\mkern-3.0mu$}_{j\in J}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{C_{j}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}[}\beta_{j};Q_{j}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}]}\Big)

and

Q=\scalebox{1.5}{$\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}\mkern-3.0mu$}_{i\in I}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{C_{i}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}[}\alpha_{i};P_{i}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}]}
,
where

(i)
\forall i,i^{\prime}\in I.\,\alpha_{i}\bowtie\alpha_{i^{\prime}}
and
\mathit{subj}\{\alpha_{i}\}=\{x\}
,
and

(ii)
\forall i\in I.\,\forall j\in J.\,\alpha_{i}\not\bowtie\beta_{j}\land x\in\mathit{fn}(\beta_{j};Q_{j})
;

2.

S=\{x,y\}

implies

P=\Big(\scalebox{1.5}{$\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}\mkern-3.0mu$}_{i\in I}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{C_{i}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}[}[x\mathbin{\leftrightarrow}y]{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}]}\Big)\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}\Big(\scalebox{1.5}{$\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}\mkern-3.0mu$}_{j\in J}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{C_{j}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}[}[x\mathbin{\leftrightarrow}z_{j}]{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}]}\Big)\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}\Big(\scalebox{1.5}{$\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}\mkern-3.0mu$}_{k\in K}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{C_{k}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}[}\alpha_{k};P_{k}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}]}\Big)

and

Q=\scalebox{1.5}{$\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}\mkern-3.0mu$}_{i\in I}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{C_{i}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}[}[x\mathbin{\leftrightarrow}y]{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}]}
,
where

(i)
\forall j\in J.\,z_{j}\not=y
,
and
(ii)
\forall k\in K.\ x\in\mathit{fn}(\alpha_{k};P_{k})\land\forall z.\ \alpha_{k}\not\bowtie[x\mathbin{\leftrightarrow}z]
.

Intuitively,
\bowtie
allows us to equate output/input prefixes with the same subject (but different object).
The rest of
Definition

3
accounts for two kinds of reduction, using
S
to discard “impossible” branches.
In case
S
is
\{x\}
(
Item

1
), it concerns a synchronization on
x
; in case
S
is
\{x,y\}
, it concerns forwarding on
x
and
y
(
Item

2
).
In both cases,
P
and
Q
contain matching prefixes on
x
, while
P
may contain additional branches with different or blocked prefixes on
x
;
x
must appear in the hole of the contexts in the additional branches in
P
(enforced with
x\in\mathit{fn}(\ldots)
), to ensure that no matching prefixes are discarded.

Example 2

Recall process
P
from
Example

1
.
To derive a synchronization with the ‘
buy
’ alternative of the case, we can use
\mathrel{\succeq_{S}}
to discard the ‘
peek
’ alternative, as follows:

\overline{s}.\mathsf{buy};\mathinner{\ldotp\kern-1.99997pt\ldotp\kern-1.99997pt\ldotp}\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}\overline{s}.\mathsf{buy};\mathinner{\ldotp\kern-1.99997pt\ldotp\kern-1.99997pt\ldotp}\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}\overline{s}.\mathsf{peek};\mathinner{\ldotp\kern-1.99997pt\ldotp\kern-1.99997pt\ldotp}~\mathrel{\succeq_{s}}~\overline{s}.\mathsf{buy};\mathinner{\ldotp\kern-1.99997pt\ldotp\kern-1.99997pt\ldotp}\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}\overline{s}.\mathsf{buy};\mathinner{\ldotp\kern-1.99997pt\ldotp\kern-1.99997pt\ldotp}
∎
