Figure 2:

Reduction semantics for
{{\mathsf{s}\pi\mkern-2.0mu}^{+}\mkern-1.0mu}
.

Reduction Rules.

Figure

2
gives the rules for the (lazy) reduction semantics, denoted
\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.38,0.61,0.27}\leadsto}}_{S}
, where the set

S
contains the names involved in the interaction. We omit the curly braces in this annotation; this way, e.g., we write ‘
\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.38,0.61,0.27}\leadsto}}_{x,y}
’ instead of ‘
\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.38,0.61,0.27}\leadsto}}_{\{x,y\}}
’.

Also, we write
\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.38,0.61,0.27}\leadsto}}_{S}^{k}
to denote a sequence of
k\geq 0
reductions.

The first six rules in
Figure

2
formalize forwarding and communication: they are defined on choices containing different D-contexts (cf.
Definition

1
), each with the same prefix but possibly different continuations; these rules preserve the non-deterministic choices.
Rule
[{\color[rgb]{0.38,0.61,0.27}\rightsquigarrow}_{\text{Id}}]
fixes
S
to the forwarder’s two names, and the other rules fix
S
to the one involved name.
In particular, Rule
[{\color[rgb]{0.38,0.61,0.27}\rightsquigarrow}_{{\mathbin{\otimes}}{\mathbin{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle{{\binampersand}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle{{\binampersand}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle{{\binampersand}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle{{\binampersand}}$}}}}}]
formalizes name communication: it involves multiple senders and multiple receivers (grouped in choices indexed by
I
and
J
, respectively). Because they proceed in lock-step, reduction leads to substitutions involving the same (fresh) name
w
; also, the scopes of the choice and the contexts enclosing the senders is extended.

Rule
[{\color[rgb]{0.38,0.61,0.27}\rightsquigarrow}_{\mathrel{\succeq_{S}}}]
is useful to derive a synchronization that discards groups of choices.

Rule
[{\color[rgb]{0.38,0.61,0.27}\rightsquigarrow}_{\nu\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}}]
allows inferring reductions when non-deterministic choices are not top-level: e.g.,
(\bm{\nu}x)\big(\overline{x}[]\mathbin{|}(\bm{\nu}y)((x();Q_{1}\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}x();Q_{2})\mathbin{|}R)\big)\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.38,0.61,0.27}\leadsto}}_{x}(\bm{\nu}y)(Q_{1}\mathbin{|}R)\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}(\bm{\nu}y)(Q_{2}\mathbin{|}R)
.

The last four rules formalize that reduction is closed under structural congruence, restriction, parallel composition, and non-deterministic choice.

As mentioned earlier, a key motivation for our work is to have non-deterministic choices that effectively enforce commitment, without a too drastic discarding of alternatives.
Next we illustrate this intended form of
gradual commitment
.

Example 3

(A Modified Movie Server)

Consider the following variant of the movie server from the introduction, where the handling of the payment is now modeled as a branch:

\mathsf{NewServer}_{s}:=s(\textit{title});s.\mathtt{case}\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\mathsf{buy}:s.\mathtt{case}\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\mathsf{card}:s(\textit{info});\overline{s}[\texttt{movie}];\overline{s}[],\\
\mathsf{cash}:\overline{s}[\texttt{movie}];\overline{s}[]\end{array}\right\},\\
\mathsf{peek}:\overline{s}[\texttt{trailer}];\overline{s}[]\end{array}\right\}

Consider a client, Eve, who cannot decide between buying ‘Oppenheimer’ or watching its trailer.
In the former case, she has two options for payment method:

\mathsf{Eve}_{s}:=\overline{s}[\texttt{Oppenheimer}];\left(\begin{array}[]{@{}rl@{}}&\overline{s}.\mathsf{buy};\overline{s}.\mathsf{card};\overline{s}[\texttt{visa}];s(\textit{movie});s();\bm{0}\\
{\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}}&\overline{s}.\mathsf{buy};\overline{s}.\mathsf{cash};s(\textit{movie});s();\bm{0}\\
{\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}}&\overline{s}.\mathsf{peek};s(\textit{link});s();\bm{0}\end{array}\right)

Let
\mathsf{Sys}^{*}:=(\bm{\nu}s)(\mathsf{NewServer}_{s}\mathbin{|}\mathsf{Eve}_{s})
.
After sending the movie’s title, Eve’s choice (buying or watching the trailer) enables gradual commitment. We have:

\mathsf{Sys}^{*}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.38,0.61,0.27}\leadsto}}_{s}^{2}(\bm{\nu}s)\big(s.\mathtt{case}\{\mathsf{card}:\ldots,\mathsf{cash}:\ldots\}\mathbin{|}(\overline{s}.\mathsf{card};\ldots\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}\overline{s}.\mathsf{cash};\ldots)\big)=:\mathsf{Sys}^{*}_{1}\\
\text{and}\mathsf{Sys}^{*}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.38,0.61,0.27}\leadsto}}_{s}^{2}(\bm{\nu}s)(\overline{s}[\texttt{trailer}];\ldots\mathbin{|}s(\textit{trailer});\ldots)=:\mathsf{Sys}^{*}_{2}

Process
\mathsf{Sys}^{*}_{1}
represents the situation for Eve after selecting
\mathsf{buy}
, in which case the third alternative (
\overline{s}.\mathsf{peek};\ldots
) can be discarded as an impossible branch.
Process
\mathsf{Sys}^{*}_{2}
represents the dual situation. From
\mathsf{Sys}^{*}_{1}
, the selection of payment method completes the commitment to one alternative; we have:

\mathsf{Sys}^{*}_{1}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.38,0.61,0.27}\leadsto}}_{s}(\bm{\nu}s)(s(\textit{info});\mathinner{\ldotp\kern-1.99997pt\ldotp\kern-1.99997pt\ldotp}\mathbin{|}\overline{s}[\texttt{visa}];\mathinner{\ldotp\kern-1.99997pt\ldotp\kern-1.99997pt\ldotp})

and

\mathsf{Sys}^{*}_{1}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.38,0.61,0.27}\leadsto}}_{s}(\bm{\nu}s)(\overline{s}[\texttt{movie}];\mathinner{\ldotp\kern-1.99997pt\ldotp\kern-1.99997pt\ldotp}\mathbin{|}s(\textit{movie});\mathinner{\ldotp\kern-1.99997pt\ldotp\kern-1.99997pt\ldotp})
.

∎

In
Appendix

0.B

we discuss an alternative
eager
semantics that commits to a single branch upon communication, as in (
1
).

2.2
Resource Control for
{{\mathsf{s}\pi\mkern-2.0mu}^{+}\mkern-1.0mu}
via Session Types

We define a session type system for
{{\mathsf{s}\pi\mkern-2.0mu}^{+}\mkern-1.0mu}
, following ‘propositions-as-sessions’
[
6
,
30
]
.
As already mentioned, in a session type system,
resources are names that perform protocols:
the
type assignment

x:A
says that
x
should conform to the protocol specified by the session type
A
.
We give the syntax of types:

\displaystyle A,B

\displaystyle::=\bm{1}\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}\bot\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}A\mathbin{\otimes}B\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}A\mathbin{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle{{\binampersand}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle{{\binampersand}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle{{\binampersand}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle{{\binampersand}}$}}}B\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}{\oplus}\{i:A\}_{i\in I}\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}{{\binampersand}}\{i:A\}_{i\in I}\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}{{\binampersand}}A\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}{\oplus}A

The units
\bm{1}
and
\bot
type closed sessions.

A\mathbin{\otimes}B
types a name that first outputs a name of type
A
and then proceeds as
B
.
Similarly,
A\mathbin{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle{{\binampersand}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle{{\binampersand}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle{{\binampersand}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle{{\binampersand}}$}}}B
types a name that inputs a name of type
A
and then proceeds as
B
.
Types
{\oplus}\{i:A_{i}\}_{i\in I}
and
{{\binampersand}}\{i:A_{i}\}_{i\in I}
are given to names that can select and offer a labeled choice, respectively.
Then,
{{\binampersand}}A
is the type of a name that
may produce
a behavior of type
A
, or fail; dually,
{\oplus}A
types a name that
may consume
a behavior of type
A
.

For any type
A
we denote its
dual
as
\overline{A}
.
Intuitively, dual types serve to avoid communication errors: the type at one end of a channel is the dual of the type at the opposite end.
Duality is an involution, defined as follows:

\displaystyle\overline{\bm{1}}

\displaystyle=\bot

\displaystyle\overline{A\mathbin{\otimes}B}

\displaystyle=\overline{A}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle{{\binampersand}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle{{\binampersand}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle{{\binampersand}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle{{\binampersand}}$}}}\overline{B}

\displaystyle\overline{{\oplus}\{i:A_{i}\}_{i\in I}}

\displaystyle={{\binampersand}}\{i:\overline{A_{i}}\}_{i\in I}

\displaystyle\overline{{{\binampersand}}A}

\displaystyle={\oplus}\overline{A}

\displaystyle\overline{\bot}

\displaystyle=\bm{1}

\displaystyle\overline{A\mathbin{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle{{\binampersand}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle{{\binampersand}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle{{\binampersand}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle{{\binampersand}}$}}}B}

\displaystyle=\overline{A}\mathbin{\otimes}\overline{B}

\displaystyle\overline{{{\binampersand}}\{i:A_{i}\}_{i\in I}}

\displaystyle={\oplus}\{i:\overline{A_{i}}\}_{i\in I}

\displaystyle\overline{{\oplus}A}

\displaystyle={{\binampersand}}\overline{A}

Judgments are of the form
P\vdash\Gamma
, where
P
is a process and
\Gamma
is a context, a collection of type assignments.
In writing
\Gamma,x:A
, we assume
x\notin\mathit{dom}(\Gamma)
.
We write
\mathit{dom}(\Gamma)
to denote the set of names appearing in
\Gamma
.
We write
{\binampersand}\Gamma
to denote that
\forall x:A\in\Gamma.~\exists A^{\prime}.~A={\binampersand}A^{\prime}
.

{\text{\scriptsize[{Tcut}]}}~\frac{P\vdash\Gamma,x{:}A\hskip 9.24994ptQ\vdash\Delta,x{:}\overline{A}}{(\bm{\nu}x)(P\mathbin{|}Q)\vdash\Gamma,\Delta}\text{\scriptsize[{Tmix}]}~\frac{P\vdash\Gamma\hskip 9.24994ptQ\vdash\Delta}{P\mathbin{|}Q\vdash\Gamma,\Delta}\text{\scriptsize[{T$\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}$}]}~\frac{P\vdash\Gamma\hskip 9.24994ptQ\vdash\Gamma}{P\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}Q\vdash\Gamma}\text{\scriptsize[{Tempty}]}~\frac{}{\bm{0}\vdash\emptyset}\text{\scriptsize[{Tid}]}~\frac{}{[x\mathbin{\leftrightarrow}y]\vdash x{:}A,y{:}\overline{A}}\text{\scriptsize[{T$\bm{1}$}]}~\frac{}{\overline{x}[]\vdash x{:}\bm{1}}\text{\scriptsize[{T$\bot$}]}~\frac{P\vdash\Gamma}{x();P\vdash\Gamma,x{:}\bot}\text{\scriptsize[{T$\mathbin{\otimes}$}]}~\frac{P\vdash\Gamma,y{:}A\hskip 9.24994ptQ\vdash\Delta,x{:}B}{\overline{x}[y];(P\mathbin{|}Q)\vdash\Gamma,\Delta,x{:}A\mathbin{\otimes}B}\text{\scriptsize[{T$\mathbin{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle{{\binampersand}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle{{\binampersand}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle{{\binampersand}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle{{\binampersand}}$}}}$}]}~\frac{P\vdash\Gamma,y{:}A,x{:}B}{x(y);P\vdash\Gamma,x{:}A\mathbin{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle{{\binampersand}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle{{\binampersand}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle{{\binampersand}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle{{\binampersand}}$}}}B}\text{\scriptsize[{T$\oplus$}]}~\frac{P\vdash\Gamma,x{:}A_{j}\hskip 9.24994ptj\in I}{\overline{x}.j;P\vdash\Gamma,x{:}{\oplus}\{i:A_{i}\}_{i\in I}}\text{\scriptsize[{T${\binampersand}$}]}~\frac{\forall i\in I.~P_{i}\vdash\Gamma,x{:}A_{i}}{x.\mathtt{case}\{i:P_{i}\}_{i\in I}\vdash\Gamma,x{:}{{\binampersand}}\{i:A_{i}\}_{i\in I}}\text{\scriptsize[{T${{\binampersand}}\mathtt{some}$}]}~\frac{P\vdash\Gamma,x{:}A}{\overline{x}.\mathtt{some};P\vdash\Gamma,x{:}{{\binampersand}}A}\text{\scriptsize[{T${{\binampersand}}\mathtt{none}$}]}~\frac{}{\overline{x}.\mathtt{none}\vdash x{:}{{\binampersand}}A}\text{\scriptsize[{T${\oplus}\mathtt{some}$}]}~\frac{P\vdash{{\binampersand}}\Gamma,x{:}A}{{x.\mathtt{some}}_{\mathit{dom}(\Gamma)};P\vdash{{\binampersand}}\Gamma,x{:}{\oplus}A}

Figure 3:
Typing rules for
{{\mathsf{s}\pi\mkern-2.0mu}^{+}\mkern-1.0mu}
.

Figure

3
gives the typing rules: they correspond to the rules in Curry-Howard interpretations of classical linear logic as session types (cf. Wadler
[
30
]
), with the rules for
{{\binampersand}}A
and
{\oplus}A
extracted from
[
5
]
, and the additional Rule
[
T
\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}
]
for non-confluent non-deterministic choice, which modifies the confluent rule in
[
5
]
.

Most rules follow
[
30
]
, so we focus on those related to non-determinism.
Rule
[
T
{{\binampersand}}\mathtt{some}
]
types a process with a name whose behavior can be provided, while Rule
[
T
{{\binampersand}}\mathtt{none}
]
types a name whose behavior cannot.
Rule
[
T
{\oplus}\mathtt{some}
]
types a process with a name
x
whose behavior may not be available.
If the behavior is not available, all the sessions in the process must be canceled; hence, the rule requires all names to be typed under the
{{\binampersand}}A
monad.

Rule
[
T
\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}
]
types our new non-deterministic choice operator; the branches must be typable under the same typing context.
Hence, all branches denote the same sessions, which may be implemented differently.
In context of a synchronization, branches that are kept are able to synchronize, whereas the discarded branches are not; nonetheless, the remaining branches still represent different implementations of the same sessions.
Compared to the rule for non-determinism in
[
5
]
, we do not require processes to be typable under the
{{\binampersand}}A
monad.

Example 4

Consider again process
\mathsf{Eve}_{s}
from
Example

3
.
The three branches of the non-deterministic choice give
different implementations of the same session
: assuming primitive, self-dual data types
\mathtt{C}
,
\mathtt{M}
, and
\mathtt{L}
,
all three branches on
s
are typable by
\oplus\big\{\mathsf{buy}:\oplus\{\mathsf{card}:\mathtt{C}\mathbin{\otimes}\mathtt{M}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle{{\binampersand}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle{{\binampersand}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle{{\binampersand}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle{{\binampersand}}$}}}\bot,\mathsf{cash}:\mathtt{M}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle{{\binampersand}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle{{\binampersand}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle{{\binampersand}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle{{\binampersand}}$}}}\bot\},\mathsf{peek}:\mathtt{L}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle{{\binampersand}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle{{\binampersand}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle{{\binampersand}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle{{\binampersand}}$}}}\bot\big\}
.
∎

Example 5

(Unavailable Movies)

Consider now a modified movie server, which offers movies that may not be yet available.
We specify this server using non-deterministic choice and non-deterministically available sessions:

\mathsf{BuyServ}_{s}:=s(\textit{title});(\overline{s}.\mathtt{none}\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}\overline{s}.\mathtt{some};s(\textit{paym});\overline{s}[\texttt{movie}];\overline{s}[])\vdash s:\mathtt{T}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle{{\binampersand}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle{{\binampersand}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle{{\binampersand}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle{{\binampersand}}$}}}\big({\binampersand}(\mathtt{P}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle{{\binampersand}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle{{\binampersand}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle{{\binampersand}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle{{\binampersand}}$}}}\mathtt{M}\mathbin{\otimes}\bm{1})\big),

where
\mathtt{T},\mathtt{P},\mathtt{M}
denote primitive, self-dual data-types.
While the branch ‘
\overline{s}.\mathtt{none}
’ signals that the movie is not available, the branch ‘
\overline{s}.\mathtt{some};...
’ performs the expected protocol.
We now define a client Ada who buys a movie for Tim, using session
s
; Ada only forwards it to him (using session
u
) if it is actually available:

\displaystyle\mathsf{Ada}_{s,u}

\displaystyle:=\overline{s}[\texttt{Barbie}];{s.\mathtt{some}}_{u};\overline{s}[\texttt{visa}];s(\textit{movie});s();\overline{u}.\mathtt{some};\overline{u}[\textit{movie}];\overline{u}[]

\displaystyle\hphantom{:= }\vdash s:\mathtt{T}\mathbin{\otimes}\big(\oplus(\mathtt{P}\mathbin{\otimes}\mathtt{M}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle{{\binampersand}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle{{\binampersand}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle{{\binampersand}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle{{\binampersand}}$}}}\bot)\big),u:{\binampersand}(\mathtt{M}\mathbin{\otimes}\bm{1})

\displaystyle\mathsf{Tim}_{u}

\displaystyle:={u.\mathtt{some}};u(\textit{movie});u();\bm{0}\vdash u:\oplus(\mathtt{M}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle{{\binampersand}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle{{\binampersand}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle{{\binampersand}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle{{\binampersand}}$}}}\bm{1})

Let
\mathsf{BuySys}:=(\bm{\nu}s)\big(\mathsf{BuyServ}_{s}\mathbin{|}(\bm{\nu}u)(\mathsf{Ada}_{s,u}\mathbin{|}\mathsf{Tim}_{u})\big)
.
Depending on whether the server has the movie “Barbie” available, we have the following reductions:

\mathsf{BuySys}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.38,0.61,0.27}\leadsto}}_{s}^{2}(\bm{\nu}u)(\overline{u}.\mathtt{none}\mathbin{|}\mathsf{Tim}_{u}\mkern-2.0mu)~~\text{or}~~\mathsf{BuySys}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.38,0.61,0.27}\leadsto}}_{s}^{5}(\bm{\nu}u)(\overline{u}.\mathtt{some}{;}\mkern-2.0mu\mathinner{\ldotp\kern-1.99997pt\ldotp\kern-1.99997pt\ldotp}\mathbin{|}\mathsf{Tim}_{u}\mkern-2.0mu)

Our type system ensures
session fidelity
and
communication safety
, but not confluence:
the former says that processes correctly follow their ascribed session protocols, and the latter that no communication errors/mismatches occur.
Both properties follow from the fact that typing is consistent across structural congruence and reduction.
See
Section

0.E.2
for details.

Theorem 2.1

(Type Preservation)

If
P\vdash\Gamma
, then both
P\equiv Q
and
P\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.38,0.61,0.27}\leadsto}}_{S}Q
(for any
Q
and
S
) imply
Q\vdash\Gamma
.

Another important, if often elusive, property in session types is
deadlock-freedom
, which ensures that processes can reduce as long as they are not inactive.
Our type system satisfies deadlock-freedom for processes with fully connected names, i.e., typable under the empty context.
See
Section

0.E.2
for details.

Theorem 2.2

(Deadlock-freedom)

If
P\vdash\emptyset
and
P\not\equiv\bm{0}
, then there are
Q
and
S
such that
P\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.38,0.61,0.27}\leadsto}}_{S}Q
.

3
A Non-deterministic Resource
\lambda
-calculus

We present
\lambda_{\mathtt{C}}^{\ell}
, a resource
\lambda
-calculus
with non-determinism and lazy evaluation.
In
\lambda_{\mathtt{C}}^{\ell}
, non-determinism is non-confluent and
implicit
, as it arises from the fetching of terms from bags of
linear
resources.
This is different from
{{\mathsf{s}\pi\mkern-2.0mu}^{+}\mkern-1.0mu}
, where the choice operator ‘
\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}
’
specifies non-determinism
explicitly
.
A mismatch between the number of variable occurrences and the size of the bag induces
failure
.

In
\lambda_{\mathtt{C}}^{\ell}
, the
sharing
construct
M[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\leftarrow x]
,
expresses that

x
may be used in
M
under “aliases”
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
.
Hence, it atomizes
n
occurrences of
x
in
M
, via an explicit pointer to
n
variables.
This way, e.g.,
the
\lambda
-term
\lambda x.(x\ x)
is expressed in
\lambda_{\mathtt{C}}^{\ell}
as
\lambda x.(x_{1}\lbag x_{2}\rbag[x_{1},x_{2}\leftarrow x])
, where
\lbag x_{2}\rbag
is a bag containing
x_{2}
.

\displaystyle M,N,L::=~

\displaystyle x

variable

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt{}\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}M\langle\!\langle\raisebox{1.93748pt}{\small$C$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle

intermediate subst.

\displaystyle\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}~

\displaystyle(M\ C)

application

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt{}\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}M{\langle\!|}C/\widetilde{x}{|\!\rangle}

explicit subst.

\displaystyle\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}~

\displaystyle\lambda x.M

abstraction

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt{}\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}\mathtt{fail}^{\tilde{x}}

failure

\displaystyle\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}~

\displaystyle M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]

sharing

\displaystyle C,D::=~

\displaystyle\mathtt{1}\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}\lbag M\rbag\cdot\,C

bag

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}::=~

\displaystyle[\cdot]\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}\mathcal{C}[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}(\mathcal{C}\ C)\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}\mathcal{C}{\langle\!|}C/\widetilde{x}{|\!\rangle}

context

Figure 4:
Syntax of
\lambda_{\mathtt{C}}^{\ell}
: terms, bags, and contexts.

3.1
Syntax and Reduction Semantics

Syntax.

We use
x,y,z,\ldots
for variables, and write
\widetilde{x}
to denote a finite sequence of pairwise distinct
x_{i}
’s, with length
|\widetilde{x}|
.

Figure

4
gives the syntax of terms (
M,N,L
) and bags (
C,D
).
The empty bag is denoted
\mathtt{1}
.
We use
C_{i}
to denote the
i
-th term in
C
, and
\mathsf{size}(C)
denotes the number of elements in
C
.
To ease readability, we often write, e.g.,
\lbag N_{1},N_{2}\rbag

as a shorthand notation for

\lbag N_{1}\rbag\cdot\lbag N_{2}\rbag
.

In
M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]
, we say that
\widetilde{x}
are the
shared variables
and that
x
is the
sharing variable
.
We require for each
x_{i}\in\widetilde{x}
: (i)
x_{i}
occurs exactly once in
M
; (ii)
x_{i}
is not a sharing variable.
The sequence
\widetilde{x}
can be empty:

M[\leftarrow x]
means that
x
does not share any variables in
M
.
Sharing binds the shared variables in the term.

An abstraction
\lambda x.M
binds
occurrences of
x
in
M
.
Application
(M\ C)
is as usual.
The term
M{\langle\!|}C/\widetilde{x}{|\!\rangle}
is the
explicit substitution
of a bag
C
for
\widetilde{x}
in
M
.
We require
\mathsf{size}(C)=|\widetilde{x}|
and for each
x_{i}\in\widetilde{x}
: (i)
x_{i}
occurs in
M
; (ii)
x_{i}
is not a sharing variable; (iii)
x_{i}
cannot occur in another explicit substitution in
M
.
The term
M\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$C$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle
denotes an intermediate explicit substitution that does not (necessarily) satisfy the conditions for explicit substitutions.

The term
\mathtt{fail}^{\tilde{x}}
denotes failure; the variables in
\widetilde{x}
are “dangling” resources, which cannot be accounted for after failure.
We write
\mathsf{fv}(M)
to denote the free variables of
M
, defined as expected.
Term
M
is
closed
if
\mathsf{fv}(M)=\emptyset
.

\frac{}{(\lambda x.M)C\mathbin{\longrightarrow}M\langle\!\langle\raisebox{1.93748pt}{\small$C$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{$\mathtt{[RS{:}Beta]}$}\frac{\mathsf{size}(C)=|\widetilde{x}|\hskip 9.24994ptM\not=\mathtt{fail}^{\tilde{y}}}{(M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x])\langle\!\langle\raisebox{1.93748pt}{\small$C$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle\mathbin{\longrightarrow}M{\langle\!|}C/\widetilde{x}{|\!\rangle}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{$\mathtt{[RS{:}Ex\text{-}Sub]}$}\frac{M\mathbin{\longrightarrow}N}{\mathcal{C}[M]\mathbin{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{C}[N]}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{$\mathtt{[RS:TCont]}$}\frac{\mathsf{head}(M)={x}_{j}\hskip 9.24994pt0<i\leq\mathsf{size}(C)}{M{\langle\!|}C/\widetilde{x},x_{j}{|\!\rangle}\mathbin{\longrightarrow}M\{C_{i}/x_{j}\}{\langle\!|}(C\setminus C_{i})/\widetilde{x}{|\!\rangle}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{$\mathtt{[RS{:}Fetch^{\ell}]}$}\frac{\mathsf{size}(C)\neq|\widetilde{x}|\hskip 9.24994pt\widetilde{y}=(\mathsf{fv}(M)\setminus\{\widetilde{x}\})\cup\mathsf{fv}(C)}{(M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x])\langle\!\langle\raisebox{1.93748pt}{\small$C$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle\mathbin{\longrightarrow}\mathtt{fail}^{\tilde{y}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{$\mathtt{[RS{:}Fail^{\ell}]}$}\frac{\widetilde{y}=\mathsf{fv}(C)}{\mathtt{fail}^{\tilde{x}}\ C\mathbin{\longrightarrow}\mathtt{fail}^{\tilde{x}\cup\widetilde{y}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{$\mathtt{[RS{:}Cons_{1}]}$}\frac{\mathsf{size}(C)=|{\widetilde{x}}|\hskip 9.24994pt\widetilde{z}=\mathsf{fv}(C)}{(\mathtt{fail}^{{\tilde{x}}\cup\tilde{y}}[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x])\langle\!\langle\raisebox{1.93748pt}{\small$C$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle\mathbin{\longrightarrow}\mathtt{fail}^{\tilde{y}\cup\widetilde{z}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{$\mathtt{[RS{:}Cons_{2}]}$}\frac{\widetilde{z}=\mathsf{fv}(C)}{\mathtt{fail}^{\tilde{y}\cup\tilde{x}}{\langle\!|}C/\widetilde{x}{|\!\rangle}\mathbin{\longrightarrow}\mathtt{fail}^{\tilde{y}\cup\widetilde{z}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{$\mathtt{[RS{:}Cons_{3}]}$}

where
\mathsf{head}(M)
is defined as follows:

\displaystyle\mathsf{head}(x)

\displaystyle=x

\displaystyle\mathsf{head}(\lambda x.M)

\displaystyle=\lambda x.M

\displaystyle\mathsf{head}((M\ C))

\displaystyle=\mathsf{head}(M)

\displaystyle\mathsf{head}(\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{x}})

\displaystyle=\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{x}}

\displaystyle\mathsf{head}(M\langle\!\langle\raisebox{1.93748pt}{\small$C$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle)

\displaystyle=M\langle\!\langle\raisebox{1.93748pt}{\small$C$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle

\displaystyle\mathsf{head}(M{\langle\!|}C/\widetilde{x}{|\!\rangle})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{head}(M)

\displaystyle\mathsf{head}(M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x])=\begin{cases}x&\text{$\mathsf{head}(M)=y$ and $y\in{\widetilde{x}}$}\\
\mathsf{head}(M)&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

Figure 5:
Reduction rules for
\lambda_{\mathtt{C}}^{\ell}
.

Semantics.

Figure

5
gives the reduction semantics, denoted
\mathbin{\longrightarrow}
, and the
head variable
of term
M
, denoted
\mathsf{head}(M)
.
Rule
\mathtt{[RS:Beta]}
induces an intermediate substitution.
Rule
\mathtt{[RS:Ex{\text{-}}Sub]}
reduces an intermediate substitution to an explicit substitution, provided the size of the bag equals the number of shared variables.
In case of a mismatch, the term evolves into failure via Rule
\mathtt{[RS:Fail^{\ell}]}
.

An explicit substitution
M{\langle\!|}C/\widetilde{x}{|\!\rangle}
, where the head variable of
M
is
x_{j}\in\widetilde{x}
, reduces via Rule
\mathtt{[R:Fetch^{\ell}]}
.
The rule extracts a
C_{i}
from
C
(for some
0<i\leq\mathsf{size}(C)
) and substitutes it for
x_{j}
in
M
; this is how fetching induces a non-deterministic choice between
\mathsf{size}(C)
possible reductions.
Rules
\mathtt{[RS:Cons_{j}]}
for
j\in\{1,2,3\}
consume terms when they meet failure.
Finally, Rule
\mathtt{[RS:TCont]}
closes reduction under contexts.
The following example illustrates reduction.

Example 6

Consider the term
M_{0}=(\lambda x.x_{1}\lbag x_{2}\lbag x_{3}\ \mathtt{1}\rbag\rbag[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x])\ \lbag\mathtt{fail}^{\emptyset},y,I\,\rbag
,
where
I=\lambda x.(x_{1}[x_{1}\leftarrow x])
and
\widetilde{x}=x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}
.
First,
M_{0}
evolves into an intermediate substitution (
2
).
The bag can provide for all shared variables, so it then evolves into an explicit substitution (
3
):

\displaystyle M_{0}

\displaystyle\mathbin{\longrightarrow}(x_{1}\!\lbag\!x_{2}\!\lbag\!x_{3}\ \mathtt{1}\!\rbag\!\rbag[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x])\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\lbag\mathtt{fail}^{\emptyset},y,I\rbag$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle

(2)

\displaystyle\mathbin{\longrightarrow}(x_{1}\!\lbag\!x_{2}\!\lbag\!x_{3}\ \mathtt{1}\!\rbag\!\rbag){\langle\!|}\!\lbag\!\mathtt{fail}^{\emptyset},y,I\!\rbag\!/\widetilde{x}{|\!\rangle}=M

(3)

Since
\mathsf{head}(M)=x_{1}
, one of the three elements of the bag will be substituted.

M
represents a non-deterministic choice between the following three reductions:

\displaystyle\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin]{30.0}{$\mathbin{\longrightarrow}$}}

\displaystyle~(\mathtt{fail}^{\emptyset}\lbag x_{2}\lbag x_{3}\ \mathtt{1}\rbag\rbag){\langle\!|}\lbag y,I\rbag/x_{2},x_{3}{|\!\rangle}=N_{1}

\displaystyle M~\mathbin{\longrightarrow}

\displaystyle~(y\lbag x_{2}\lbag x_{3}\ \mathtt{1}\rbag\rbag){\langle\!|}\lbag\mathtt{fail}^{\emptyset},I\rbag/x_{2},x_{3}{|\!\rangle}=N_{2}

\displaystyle\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin]{-30.0}{$\mathbin{\longrightarrow}$}}

\displaystyle~(I\lbag x_{2}\lbag x_{3}\ \mathtt{1}\rbag\rbag){\langle\!|}\lbag\mathtt{fail}^{\emptyset},y\rbag/x_{2},x_{3}{|\!\rangle}=N_{3}

∎

3.2
Resource Control for
\lambda_{\mathtt{C}}^{\ell}
via Intersection Types

Our type system for
\lambda_{\mathtt{C}}^{\ell}
is based on non-idempotent intersection types.
As in prior works
[
19
,
4
]
, intersection types account for available resources in bags, which are unordered and have all the same type.
Because we admit the term
\mathtt{fail}^{\tilde{x}}
as typable, we say that our system enforces
well-formedness
rather than
well-typedness
.
As we will see, well-typed terms form the sub-class of well-formed terms that does not include
\mathtt{fail}^{\tilde{x}}
(see the text after
Theorem

3.1
).

Strict types (
\sigma,\tau,\delta
) and multiset types (
\pi,\zeta
) are defined as follows:

\displaystyle\sigma,\tau,\delta::=~

\displaystyle\mathbf{unit}\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}\pi\rightarrow\sigma\qquad\pi,\zeta::=\bigwedge_{i\in I}\sigma_{i}\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}\omega

Given a non-empty
I
, multiset types
\bigwedge_{i\in I}\sigma_{i}
are given to bags of size
|I|
.
This operator is associative, commutative, and non-idempotent (i.e.,
\sigma\wedge\sigma\neq\sigma
), with identity
\omega
.
Notation
\sigma^{k}
stands for
\sigma\wedge\cdots\wedge\sigma
(
k
times, if
k>0
) or
\omega
(if
k=0
).

Judgments have the form
\Gamma\vDash M:\tau
, with contexts defined as follows:

\displaystyle\Gamma,\Delta

\displaystyle::=\text{-}\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}\Gamma,x:\pi\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}\Gamma,x:\sigma

where
-
denotes the empty context.
We write
\mathit{dom}(\Gamma)
for the set of variables in
\Gamma
.
For
\Gamma,x:\pi
, we assume
x\not\in\mathit{dom}(\Gamma)
.
To avoid ambiguities, we write
x:\sigma^{1}
to denote that the assignment involves a multiset type, rather than a strict type.
Given
\Gamma
, its
core context

\Gamma^{\downarrow}
concerns variables with types different from
\omega
; it is defined as

\Gamma^{\downarrow}=\{x:\pi\in\Gamma\,|\,\pi\not=\omega\}
.

Definition 4

(Well-formedness in
\lambda_{\mathtt{C}}^{\ell}
)
.

A term
M
is
well-formed
if there exists a context
\Gamma
and a type
\tau
such that the rules in
Figure

6
entail
\Gamma\vDash M:\tau
.

\frac{}{{x}:\sigma\vDash{x}:\sigma}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{$\mathtt{[FS{:}var^{\ell}]}$}\frac{}{\text{-}\vDash\mathtt{1}:\omega}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{$\mathtt{[FS{:}\mathtt{1}^{\ell}]}$}\frac{\Gamma\vDash N:\sigma\hskip 9.24994pt\Delta\vDash C:\sigma^{k}}{\Gamma,\Delta\vDash\lbag N\rbag\cdot C:\sigma^{k+1}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{$\mathtt{[FS{:}bag^{\ell}]}$}\frac{\mathit{dom}(\Gamma^{\downarrow})=\widetilde{x}}{\Gamma^{\downarrow}\vDash\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{x}}:\tau}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{$\mathtt{[FS{:}fail]}$}\frac{\Gamma\vDash M:\tau}{\Gamma,{x}:\omega\vDash M[\leftarrow x]:\tau}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{$\mathtt{[FS{:}weak]}$}\frac{\Gamma,{x}_{1}:\sigma,\ldots,{x}_{k}:\sigma\vDash M:\tau\hskip 9.24994ptk\not=0}{\Gamma,{x}:\sigma^{k}\vDash M[{x}_{1},\ldots,{x}_{k}\leftarrow x]:\tau}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{$\mathtt{[FS{:}shar]}$}\frac{\Gamma,{x}:\sigma^{k}\vDash M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]:\tau}{\Gamma\vDash\lambda x.(M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]):\sigma^{k}\rightarrow\tau}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{$\mathtt{[FS{:}abs\text{-}sh]}$}\frac{\Gamma\vDash M:\sigma^{j}\rightarrow\tau\hskip 9.24994pt\Delta\vDash C:\sigma^{k}\hskip 9.24994pt}{\Gamma,\Delta\vDash M\ C:\tau}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{$\mathtt{[FS{:}app]}$}\frac{\Gamma,{x}:\sigma^{j}\vDash M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]:\tau\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\Delta\vDash C:\sigma^{k}}{\Gamma,\Delta\vDash(M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x])\langle\!\langle\raisebox{1.93748pt}{\small$C$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle:\tau}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{$\mathtt{[FS{:}Esub]}$}\frac{\Gamma,x_{1}:\sigma,\cdots,x_{k}:\sigma\vDash M:\tau\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\Delta\vDash C:\sigma^{k}}{\Gamma,\Delta\vDash M{\langle\!|}C/x_{1},\cdots,x_{k}{|\!\rangle}:\tau}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{$\mathtt{[FS{:}Esub^{\ell}]}$}

Figure 6:
Well-Formedness Rules for
\lambda_{\mathtt{C}}^{\ell}
.

In
Figure

6
,
Rule
\mathtt{[FS:var^{\ell}]}
types variables.
Rule
\mathtt{[FS:\mathtt{1}^{\ell}]}
types the empty bag with
\omega
.
Rule
\mathtt{[FS:bag^{\ell}]}
types the concatenation of bags.
Rule
\mathtt{[FS:\mathtt{fail}]}
types the term
\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{x}}
with a strict type
\tau
, provided that the domain of the core context coincides with
\widetilde{x}
(i.e., no variable in
\widetilde{x}
is typed with
\omega
).
Rule
\mathtt{[FS:weak]}
types
M[\leftarrow x]
by weakening the context with
x:\omega
.
Rule
\mathtt{[FS:shar]}
types
M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]
with
\tau
, provided that there are assignments to the shared variables in
\widetilde{x}
.

Rule
\mathtt{[FS:abs{\text{-}}sh]}
types an abstraction
\lambda x.(M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x])
with
\sigma^{k}\to\tau
, provided that
M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]:\tau
can be entailed from an assignment
x:\sigma^{k}
.
Rule
\mathtt{[FS:app]}
types
(M\ C)
, provided that
M
has type
\sigma^{j}\to\tau
and
C
has type
\sigma^{k}
. Note that, unlike usual intersection type systems,
j
and
k
may differ.
Rule
\mathtt{[FS:Esub]}
types the intermediate substitution of a bag
C
of type
\sigma^{k}
, provided that
x
has type
\sigma^{j}
; again,
j
and
k
may differ.
Rule
\mathtt{[FS:Esub^{\ell}]}
types
M{\langle\!|}C/\widetilde{x}{|\!\rangle}
as long as
C
has type
\sigma^{|\widetilde{x}|}
, and each
x_{i}\in\widetilde{x}
is of type
\sigma
.

Well-formed terms satisfy subject reduction (SR), whereas
well-typed
terms, defined below, satisfy also subject expansion (SE).
See
\lx@sectionsign

0.F.1
and
\lx@sectionsign

0.F.2
for details.

Theorem 3.1

(SR in
\lambda_{\mathtt{C}}^{\ell}
)

If
\Gamma\vDash M:\tau
and
M\mathbin{\longrightarrow}M^{\prime}
, then
\Gamma\vDash M^{\prime}:\tau
.

From our system for well-formedness we can extract a system for
well-typed
terms, which do not include
\mathtt{fail}^{\tilde{x}}
.
Judgments for well-typedness are denoted
\Gamma\vdash M:\tau
, with rules copied from
Figure

6
(the rule name prefix
FS
is replaced with
TS
), with the following modifications:
(i) Rule
\mathtt{[TS{:}fail]}
is removed; (ii) Rules
\mathtt{[TS{:}app]}
and
\mathtt{[TS{:}Esub]}
are modified to disallow a mismatch between variables and resources, i.e., multiset types should match in size.
Well-typed terms are also well-formed, and thus satisfy SR.
Moreover, as a consequence of adopting (non-idempotent) intersection types, they also satisfy SE:

Theorem 3.2

(SE in
\lambda_{\mathtt{C}}^{\ell}
)

If
\Gamma\vdash M^{\prime}:\tau
and
M\mathbin{\longrightarrow}M^{\prime}
, then
\Gamma\vdash M:\tau
.

4
A Typed Translation of
\lambda_{\mathtt{C}}^{\ell}
into
{{\mathsf{s}\pi\mkern-2.0mu}^{+}\mkern-1.0mu}

While
{{\mathsf{s}\pi\mkern-2.0mu}^{+}\mkern-1.0mu}
features non-deterministic choice,

\lambda_{\mathtt{C}}^{\ell}
is a prototypical programming language in which implicit non-determinism
implements fetching of resources.
Resources are controlled using different type systems (session types in
{{\mathsf{s}\pi\mkern-2.0mu}^{+}\mkern-1.0mu}
, intersection types in
\lambda_{\mathtt{C}}^{\ell}
).
To reconcile these differences and
illustrate the potential of
{{\mathsf{s}\pi\mkern-2.0mu}^{+}\mkern-1.0mu}
to precisely model non-determinism as found in realistic programs/protocols,
we give a translation of
\lambda_{\mathtt{C}}^{\ell}
into
{{\mathsf{s}\pi\mkern-2.0mu}^{+}\mkern-1.0mu}
. This translation preserves types (
Theorem

4.1
) and respects well-known criteria for dynamic correctness
[
11
,
22
,
23
]
(
Theorem

4.2
).

The Translation.

Given a
\lambda_{\mathtt{C}}^{\ell}
-term
M
, its translation into
{{\mathsf{s}\pi\mkern-2.0mu}^{+}\mkern-1.0mu}
is denoted
{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{u}
and given in
Figure

7
.
As usual, every variable
x
in
M
becomes a name
x
in process
{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{u}
, where
name
u
provides the behavior of
M
.
A peculiarity is that, to handle failures in
\lambda_{\mathtt{C}}^{\ell}
,
u
is a non-deterministically available session: the translated term can be available or not, as signaled by prefixes
\overline{u}.\mathtt{some}
and
\overline{u}.\mathtt{none}
, respectively.
As a result, reductions from
{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{u}
include synchronizations that codify
M
’s behavior but also synchronizations that confirm a session’s availability.

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}{x}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{u}

\displaystyle=\overline{x}.\mathtt{some};[x\mathbin{\leftrightarrow}u]

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}\lambda x.M{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{u}

\displaystyle=\overline{u}.\mathtt{some};u(x);{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{u}

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}(M\,C){\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{u}

\displaystyle=(\bm{\nu}v)({{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{v}\mathbin{|}{v.\mathtt{some}}_{u,\mathsf{lfv}(C)};\overline{v}[x];({{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}C{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{x}\mathbin{|}[v\mathbin{\leftrightarrow}u]))

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}M\langle\!\langle\raisebox{1.93748pt}{\small$C$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{u}

\displaystyle=(\bm{\nu}x)({{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{u}\mathbin{|}{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}C{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{x})

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}\lbag N\rbag\cdot~C{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{{x}}

\displaystyle={{x}.\mathtt{some}}_{\mathsf{fv}(C),\mathsf{fv}(N)};x(y_{i});{{x}.\mathtt{some}}_{y_{i},\mathsf{fv}(C),\mathsf{fv}(N)};\overline{{x}}.\mathtt{some};\overline{{x}}[z_{i}];

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt({z_{i}.\mathtt{some}}_{\mathsf{fv}(N)};{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}N{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{z_{i}}\mathbin{|}{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}C{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{{x}}\mathbin{|}\overline{y_{i}}.\mathtt{none})

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}{\mathtt{1}}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{{x}}

\displaystyle={{x}.\mathtt{some}}_{\emptyset};x(y_{n});(\overline{y_{n}}.\mathtt{some};\overline{y_{n}}[]\mathbin{|}{{x}.\mathtt{some}}_{\emptyset};\overline{{x}}.\mathtt{none})

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}M\!{\langle\!|}\!\lbag\!N_{1},N_{2}\!\rbag\!/x_{1},x_{2}{|\!\rangle}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{u}

\displaystyle=(\bm{\nu}z_{1})({z_{1}.\mathtt{some}}_{\mathsf{fv}(N_{1})};{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}N_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{{z_{1}}}\mathbin{|}(\bm{\nu}z_{2})({z_{2}.\mathtt{some}}_{\mathsf{fv}(N_{2})};{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}N_{2}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{{z_{2}}}

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\mathbin{|}\scalebox{1.5}{$\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}\mkern-3.0mu$}_{x_{i}\in\{x_{1},x_{2}\}}\scalebox{1.5}{$\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}\mkern-3.0mu$}_{x_{j}\in\{x_{1},x_{2}\setminus x_{i}\}}{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{u}\{z_{1}/x_{i}\}\{z_{2}/x_{j}\}))

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}M[\leftarrow x]{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{u}

\displaystyle=\overline{{x}}.\mathtt{some};\overline{{x}}[y_{i}];({y_{i}.\mathtt{some}}_{u,\mathsf{fv}(M)};y_{i}();{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{u}\mathbin{|}\overline{{x}}.\mathtt{none})

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{u}

\displaystyle=\overline{{x}}.\mathtt{some};\overline{{x}}[y_{i}];\big({y_{i}.\mathtt{some}}_{\emptyset};y_{i}();\bm{0}\mathbin{|}\overline{{x}}.\mathtt{some};{{x}.\mathtt{some}}_{u,\mathsf{fv}(M)\setminus\widetilde{x}};

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\scalebox{1.5}{$\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}\mkern-3.0mu$}_{x_{i}\in\widetilde{x}}\,{x}({x}_{i});{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}M[(\widetilde{x}{\setminus}x_{i})\leftarrow x]{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{u}\big)

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}\mathtt{fail}^{x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{u}

\displaystyle=\overline{u}.\mathtt{none}\mathbin{|}\overline{x_{1}}.\mathtt{none}\mathbin{|}\ldots\mathbin{|}\overline{x_{k}}.\mathtt{none}

Figure 7:
Translation of
\lambda_{\mathtt{C}}^{\ell}
into
{{\mathsf{s}\pi\mkern-2.0mu}^{+}\mkern-1.0mu}
.

At its core, our translation follows Milner’s.
This way, e.g., the process
{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}(\lambda x.M)\ C{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{u}
enables synchronizations between
{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}\lambda x.M{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{v}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}C{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{x}
along
name
v
, resulting in the translation of an intermediate substitution.
The
key novelty
is the role and treatment of non-determinism. Accommodating non-confluent non-determinism is non-trivial, as it entails translating explicit substitutions and sharing in
\lambda_{\mathtt{C}}^{\ell}
using the non-deterministic choice operator
\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}
in
{{\mathsf{s}\pi\mkern-2.0mu}^{+}\mkern-1.0mu}
.
Next we discuss these novel aspects, while highlighting differences with respect to a translation by Paulus et al.
[
20
]
, which is given in the confluent setting (see
\lx@sectionsign

5
).

In
Figure

7
, non-deterministic choices occur in the translations of
M{\langle\!|}C/\widetilde{x}{|\!\rangle}
(explicit substitutions) and
M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]
(non-empty sharing).
Roughly speaking, the position of
\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}
in the translation of
M{\langle\!|}C/\widetilde{x}{|\!\rangle}
represents the most desirable way of mimicking the fetching of terms from a bag.
This use of
\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}
is a central idea in our translation: as we explain below, it allows for appropriate commitment in non-deterministic choices, but also for
delayed
commitment when necessary.

For simplicity, we consider explicit substitutions
M\mkern-3.0mu{\langle\!|}C/\widetilde{x}{|\!\rangle}
where
C=\lbag\mkern-2.0muN_{1}{,}N_{2}\rbag
and
\widetilde{x}=x_{1},x_{2}
.
The translation
{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}M{\langle\!|}C/\widetilde{x}{|\!\rangle}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{u}
uses the processes
{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}N_{i}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{z_{i}}
, where each
z_{i}
is fresh.
First, each bag item confirms its behavior.
Then, a variable
x_{i}\in\widetilde{x}
is chosen non-deterministically;
we ensure that these choices consider all variables.
Note that writing
\scalebox{1.5}{$\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}\mkern-3.0mu$}_{x_{i}\in\{x_{1},x_{2}\}}\scalebox{1.5}{$\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}\mkern-3.0mu$}_{x_{j}\in\{x_{1},x_{2}\}\setminus x_{i}}
is equivalent to non-deterministically assigning
x_{i},x_{j}
to each permutation of
x_{1},x_{2}
.
The resulting choice involves
{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{u}
with
x_{i},x_{j}
substituted by
z_{1},z_{2}
.
Commitment here is triggered only via synchronizations along
z_{1}
or
z_{2}
; synchronizing with

{z_{i}.\mathtt{some}}_{\mathsf{fv}(N_{i})};{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}N_{i}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{{z_{i}}}
then represents fetching
N_{i}
from the bag.
The size of the translated term
{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}M{\langle\!|}C/\widetilde{x}{|\!\rangle}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{u}
is exponential with respect to the size of
C
.

The process

{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{u}

proceeds as follows.
First, it confirms its behavior along
x
.
Then it sends a name
y_{i}
on
x
, on which a failed reduction may be handled.
Next,
the translation confirms again its behavior along
x
and non-deterministically receives a reference to an
x_{i}\in\widetilde{x}
.
Each branch consists of
{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}M[(\widetilde{x}{\setminus}x_{i})\leftarrow x]{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{u}
.
The possible choices are permuted, represented by
\scalebox{1.5}{$\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}\mkern-3.0mu$}_{x_{i}\in\widetilde{x}}
.
Synchronizations with
{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}M[(\widetilde{x}{\setminus}x_{i})\leftarrow x]{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{u}
and bags delay commitment in this choice (we return to this point below).
The process
{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}M[\leftarrow x]{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{u}
is similar but simpler: here the name
x
fails, as it cannot take further elements to substitute.

In case of a failure (i.e., a mismatch between the size of the bag
C
and the number of variables in
M
), our translation ensures that the confirmations of
C
will not succeed.
This is how failure in
\lambda_{\mathtt{C}}^{\ell}
is correctly translated to failure in
{{\mathsf{s}\pi\mkern-2.0mu}^{+}\mkern-1.0mu}
.

Translation Correctness.

The translation is typed: intersection types in
\lambda_{\mathtt{C}}^{\ell}
are translated into session types in
{{\mathsf{s}\pi\mkern-2.0mu}^{+}\mkern-1.0mu}
(
Figure

8
).
This translation of types abstractly describes how non-deterministic fetches are codified as non-deterministic session protocols.

It is worth noting that this translation of types is the same as in
[
20
]
.
This is not surprising: as we have seen, session types effectively abstract away from the behavior of processes, as all branches of a non-deterministic choice use the same typing context.
Still, it is pleasant that the translation of types remains unchanged across different translations with
our (non-confluent) non-determinism (in
Figure

7
)
and with
confluent non-determinism (in
[
20
]
).

To state
static
correctness,
we require the following definition:

Definition 5
.

Let

\Gamma={{x}_{1}:\sigma_{1}},\hbox to10.00002pt{.\hss.\hss.},{{x}_{m}:\sigma_{m}},{{v}_{1}:\pi_{1}},\hbox to10.00002pt{.\hss.\hss.},{v}_{n}:\pi_{n}

be a context.
The translation
{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{\_}
in
Figure

8
extends to contexts
as follows:

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}\Gamma{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}={}

\displaystyle{x}_{1}:{\binampersand}\overline{{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}\sigma_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}},\cdots,{x}_{m}:{\binampersand}\overline{{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}\sigma_{m}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}},{v}_{1}:\overline{{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}\pi_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{(\sigma,i_{1})}},\cdots,{v}_{n}:\overline{{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}\pi_{n}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{(\sigma,i_{n})}}

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}\mathbf{unit}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}

\displaystyle={\binampersand}\mathbf{1}

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}\sigma^{k}\rightarrow\tau{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}

\displaystyle={\binampersand}(\overline{{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}\sigma^{k}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{(\sigma,i)}}\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}\tau{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}})

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}\sigma\wedge\pi{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{(\tau,i)}=\oplus(({\binampersand}\mathbf{1})\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}(\oplus{\binampersand}((\oplus{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}\sigma{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}})\otimes({{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}\pi{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{(\tau,i)}))))

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}\omega{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{(\sigma,i)}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\oplus(({\binampersand}\bm{1})\mathbin{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle{{\binampersand}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle{{\binampersand}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle{{\binampersand}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle{{\binampersand}}$}}}(\oplus{\binampersand}\bm{1}))&\text{if $i=0$}\\
\oplus(({\binampersand}\bm{1})\mathbin{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle{{\binampersand}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle{{\binampersand}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle{{\binampersand}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle{{\binampersand}}$}}}(\oplus\,{\binampersand}((\oplus{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}\sigma{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}})\mathbin{\otimes}({{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}\omega{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{(\sigma,i-1)}))))&\text{if $i>0$}\end{cases}

Figure 8:
Translation of intersection types into session types (cf. Def.
5
).

Well-formed terms translate into well-typed processes:

Theorem 4.1

If

\Gamma\vDash{M}:\tau
,
then

{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}{M}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{u}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}\Gamma{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}},u:{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}\tau{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}
.

To state
dynamic
correctness, we rely on established notions that (abstractly) characterize
correct translations
.
A language
{\cal L}=(L,\to)
consists of a set of terms
L
and a reduction relation
\to
on
L
.
Each language
{\cal L}
is assumed to contain a success constructor
{\checkmark\mkern-4.0mu}
.
A term
T\in L
has
success
, denoted
{T}\Downarrow{{\checkmark\mkern-4.0mu}}
, when there is a sequence of reductions (using
\to
) from
T
to a term satisfying success criteria.

Given
{\cal L}_{1}=(L_{1},\to_{1})
and
{\cal L}_{2}=(L_{2},\to_{2})
, we seek translations
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket:{L}_{1}\to{L}_{2}
that are correct: they satisfy well-known correctness criteria
[
11
,
22
,
23
]
.
We state the set of correctness criteria that determine the correctness of a translation.

Definition 6

(Correct Translation)
.

Let
\mathcal{L}_{1}=(\mathcal{M},\mathbin{\shortrightarrow}_{1})
and
\mathcal{L}_{2}=(\mathcal{P},\mathbin{\shortrightarrow}_{2})
be two languages.
Let
\asymp_{2}
be an equivalence over
\mathcal{L}_{2}
.
We use
M,M^{\prime}
(resp.
P,P^{\prime}
) to range over terms in
\mathcal{M}
(resp.
\mathcal{P}
).
Given a translation
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket:{\cal M}\to{\cal P}
, we define:

Completeness:

For every
{M},{M}^{\prime}
such that
{M}\mathbin{\shortrightarrow}_{1}^{\ast}{M}^{\prime}
, there exists
P
such that
\llbracket{M}\rrbracket\mathbin{\shortrightarrow}_{2}^{\ast}P\asymp_{2}\llbracket{M}^{\prime}\rrbracket
.

Weak Soundness:

For every
M
and
P
such that
\llbracket M\rrbracket\mathbin{\shortrightarrow}_{2}^{\ast}P
, there exist
M^{\prime}
,
P^{\prime}
such that
M\mathbin{\shortrightarrow}_{1}^{\ast}M^{\prime}
and
P\mathbin{\shortrightarrow}_{2}^{\ast}P^{\prime}\asymp_{2}\llbracket M^{\prime}\rrbracket
.

Success Sensitivity:

For every
{M}
, we have
{M}\Downarrow{{\checkmark\mkern-4.0mu}}
if and only if
{\llbracket M\rrbracket}\Downarrow{{\checkmark\mkern-4.0mu}}
.

Let us write
\Lambda
to denote the set of well-formed
\lambda_{\mathtt{C}}^{\ell}
terms, and
\Pi
for the set of all well-typed
{{\mathsf{s}\pi\mkern-2.0mu}^{+}\mkern-1.0mu}
processes, both including
{\checkmark\mkern-4.0mu}
.
We have our final result:

Theorem 4.2

(Translation correctness under
\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.38,0.61,0.27}\leadsto}}
)

The translation
{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{\_}:(\Lambda,\mathbin{\longrightarrow})\to(\Pi,\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.38,0.61,0.27}\leadsto}})
is correct (cf.
Definition

6
) using equivalence
\equiv
(
Figure

1
).

The proof of
Theorem

4.2
involves instantiating/proving each of the parts of
Def.
6

.
Among these,
weak soundness
is the most challenging to prove.
Prior work on translations of typed
\lambda
into
\pi
with confluent non-determinism
[
20
]
rely critically on
confluence to match a behavior in
\pi
with a corresponding behavior in
\lambda
.
Because in our setting confluence is lost, we must resort to a different proof.

As already discussed, our translation makes the implicit non-determinism in a
\lambda_{\mathtt{C}}^{\ell}
-term
M
explicit by adding non-deterministic choices in key points of
{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{u}
.
Our reduction
\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.38,0.61,0.27}\leadsto}}
preserves those branches that simultaneously have the same prefix available (up to
\bowtie
). In proving
weak soundness,
we exploit the fact that reduction entails delayed commitment.
To see this, consider the following terms:

\displaystyle(\bm{\nu}x)((\alpha_{1};P_{1}\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}\alpha_{2};P_{2})\mathbin{|}Q)

(4)

\displaystyle(\bm{\nu}x)(\alpha_{1};P_{1}\mathbin{|}Q)\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}(\bm{\nu}x)(\alpha_{2};P_{2}\mathbin{|}Q)

(5)

In (
4
), commitment to a choice
relies on whether
\alpha_{1}\bowtie\alpha_{2}
holds (cf.
Definition

3
).
If
\alpha_{1}\not\bowtie\alpha_{2}
, a choice is made; otherwise, commitment is delayed, and depends on
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
.
Hence, in (
4
) the possibility of committing to either branch is kept open.
In contrast, in (
5
) commitment to a choice is independent of
\alpha_{1}\bowtie\alpha_{2}
.

Our translation exploits the delayed commitment of non-determinism illustrated by (
4
) to mimic commitment to non-deterministic choices in
\lambda_{\mathtt{C}}^{\ell}
, which manifests in fetching resources from bags.
The fact that this delayed commitment preserves information about the different branches (e.g.,
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
in (
4
)) is essential to establish
weak soundness,
i.e., to match a behavior in
{{\mathsf{s}\pi\mkern-2.0mu}^{+}\mkern-1.0mu}
with a corresponding step in
\lambda_{\mathtt{C}}^{\ell}
.
In contrast, forms of non-determinism in
{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}N{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{u}
that resemble (
5
) are useful to characterize behaviors different from fetching.

5
Summary and Related Work

We studied the interplay between resource control and non-determinism in typed calculi.
We introduced
{{\mathsf{s}\pi\mkern-2.0mu}^{+}\mkern-1.0mu}
and
\lambda_{\mathtt{C}}^{\ell}
, two calculi with non-confluent non-determinism, both with type systems for resource control.
Inspired by the untyped
\pi
-calculus, non-determinism in
{{\mathsf{s}\pi\mkern-2.0mu}^{+}\mkern-1.0mu}
is lazy and explicit, with session types defined following ‘propositions-as-sessions’
[
5
]
.
In
\lambda_{\mathtt{C}}^{\ell}
, non-determinism arises in the fetching of resources, and is regulated by intersection types.
A correct translation of
\lambda_{\mathtt{C}}^{\ell}
into
{{\mathsf{s}\pi\mkern-2.0mu}^{+}\mkern-1.0mu}
precisely connects their different forms of non-determinism.

Related Work

Integrating (non-confluent) non-determinism within session types is non-trivial, as carelessly discarding branches would break typability.
Work by Caires and Pérez
[
5
]
, already mentioned, develops a confluent semantics by requiring that non-determinism is only used inside the monad
{\binampersand}A
; our non-confluent semantics drops this requirement.
This allows us to consider non-deterministic choices not possible in
[
5
]
, such as, e.g., selections of different labels.
We stress that linearity is not jeopardized: the branches of ‘
\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}
’ do not represent
different sessions
, but
different implementations
of the same sessions.

Atkey et al.
[
1
]
and Kokke et al.
[
15
]
extend ‘propositions-as-sessions’ with non-determinism. Their approaches are very different (conflation of the additives and bounded linear logic, respectively) and
support non-determinism for unrestricted names only.

Also,
[
1
,
15
]
do not connect with typed
\lambda
-calculi, as we do. Rocha and Caires also consider non-determinism, relying on confluence in
[
24
]
and on unrestricted names in
[
25
]
.
Casal et al.
[
7
,
29
]
develop a type system for
mixed sessions
(sessions with mixed choices), which can express non-determinism but does not ensure deadlock-freedom.
Ensuring deadlock-freedom by typing is a key feature of the ‘propositions-as-sessions’ approach that we adopt for
{{\mathsf{s}\pi\mkern-2.0mu}^{+}\mkern-1.0mu}
.

Our language
\lambda_{\mathtt{C}}^{\ell}
is most related to calculi by Boudol
[
3
]
, Boudol and Laneve
[
4
]
, and by Pagani and Ronchi Della Rocca
[
19
]
.
Non-determinism in the calculi in
[
3
,
4
]
is committing and implicit; their linear resources can be consumed
at most
once, rather than
exactly
once.
The work
[
19
]
considers non-committing non-determinism that is both implicit (as in
\lambda_{\mathtt{C}}^{\ell}
) and explicit (via a sum operator on terms).
Both
[
3
,
19
]
develop (non-idempotent) intersection type systems to regulate resources.
In our type system, all terms in a bag have the same type; the system in
[
19
]
does not enforce this condition.
Unlike these type systems, our system for well-formedness can type terms with a lack or an excess of resources.

Boudol and Laneve
[
4
]
and Paulus et al.
[
20
]
give translations of resource
\lambda
-calculi into
\pi
.
The translation in
[
4
]

is used to study the semantics induced upon
\lambda
-terms by a translation into
\pi
; unlike ours, it does not consider types.
As already mentioned in
\lx@sectionsign

4
, Paulus et al.
[
20
]
relate calculi with
confluent
non-determinism: a resource
\lambda
-calculus with sums on terms, and the session
\pi
-calculus from
[
5
]
.
Our translation of terms and that in
[
20
]
are very different:
while here we use non-deterministic choice to mimic the sharing construct, the translation in
[
20
]
uses it to translate bags.
Hence, our
Theorem

4.2
cannot be derived from
[
20
]
.

The last decade of work on ‘propositions-as-sessions’ has delivered insightful connections with typed
\lambda
-calculi—see, e.g.,
[
30
,
27
,
28
]
.
Excepting
[
20
]
, already discussed,
none of these works consider non-deterministic
\lambda
-calculi.

We are grateful to
the anonymous reviewers for useful comments on previous versions of this paper.
We are also grateful to
Mariangiola Dezani
for her encouragement and suggestions.

(‘Unifying Correctness for Communicating Software’)
and the EPSRC Fellowship ‘VeTSpec: Verified Trustworthy Software Specification’ (EP/R034567/1).

Appendix 0.A
Full
{{\mathsf{s}\pi\mkern-2.0mu}^{+}\mkern-1.0mu}
: Replicated Servers and Clients

Full
{{\mathsf{s}\pi\mkern-2.0mu}^{+}\mkern-1.0mu}
includes unrestricted session behaviors (replicated servers and clients), not presented in
Section

2
.
Here we discuss how to add these omitted unrestricted sessions to the system described in
Section

2
.
The proofs of Type Preservation (
Theorem

2.1
) and Deadlock-freedom (
Theorem

2.2
) in
Appendix

0.E
concern Full
{{\mathsf{s}\pi\mkern-2.0mu}^{+}\mkern-1.0mu}
.

•

To the syntax of processes in
Figure

1
(top) we add two prefixes,
{?}\overline{x}[y];P
and
{!}x(y);P
, for client requests and server definitions, respectively.
Both prefixes bind
y
in
P
.

•

Now
\mathit{fn}(P)
denotes the set of free names of
P
, including those used for unrestricted sessions.
We write
\mathit{fln}(P)
to denote the set of free linear names of
P
, and
\mathit{fpn}(P)
for the set of free non-linear names.
Note that
\mathit{fpn}(P)=\mathit{fn}(P)\setminus\mathit{fln}(P)
.

•

To the structural congruence in
Figure

1
(bottom) we add a rule that cleans up unused servers:

(\bm{\nu}x)({!}x(y);P\mathbin{|}Q)\equiv Q\quad\text{(if $x\notin\mathit{fn}(Q)$)}.

•

To the lazy semantics in
Figure

2
we add the following rule that initiates a session between a client and a copy of a server:

\displaystyle{[{\color[rgb]{0.38,0.61,0.27}\rightsquigarrow}_{{?}{!}}]}\hskip 8.8543pt

\displaystyle(\bm{\nu}x)\Big(\scalebox{1.5}{$\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}\mkern-3.0mu$}_{i\in I}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{C_{i}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}[}{?}\overline{x}[y_{i}];P_{i}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}]}\mathbin{|}\scalebox{1.5}{$\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}\mkern-3.0mu$}_{j\in J}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{D_{j}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}[}{!}x(z);Q_{j}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}]}\Big)

\displaystyle\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.38,0.61,0.27}\leadsto}}_{x}\scalebox{1.5}{$\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}\mkern-3.0mu$}_{j\in J}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{D_{j}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\Big[}(\bm{\nu}x)\Big((\bm{\nu}w)\Big(\scalebox{1.5}{$\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}\mkern-3.0mu$}_{i\in I}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{C_{i}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}[}P_{i}\{w/z\}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}]}\mathbin{|}Q_{j}\{w/z\}\Big)\mathbin{|}{!}x(z);Q_{j}\Big){\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\Big]}

•

Writing
{?}\Gamma
to denote that
\forall x\in\Gamma.~\exists A.~\Gamma(x)={?}A
, to the typing rules in
Figure

3
we add:

\text{\scriptsize[{T$?$}]}~\frac{P\vdash\Gamma,y:A}{{?}\overline{x}[y];P\vdash\Gamma,x:{?}A}\text{\scriptsize[{T$!$}]}~\frac{P\vdash{?}\Gamma,y:A}{{!}x(y);P\vdash{?}\Gamma,x:{!}A}\text{\scriptsize[{Tweaken}]}~\frac{P\vdash\Gamma}{P\vdash\Gamma,x:{?}A}\text{\scriptsize[{Tcontract}]}~\frac{P\vdash\Gamma,x:{?}A,x^{\prime}:{?}A}{P\{x/x^{\prime}\}\vdash\Gamma,x:{?}A}

Moreover, we replace Rule
[
T
{\oplus}\mathtt{some}
]
with the following:

\text{[{T${\oplus}\mathtt{some}$}]}~\frac{P\vdash{{\binampersand}}\Gamma,{?}\Delta,x:A}{{x.\mathtt{some}}_{\mathit{dom}(\Gamma)};P\vdash{{\binampersand}}\Gamma,{?}\Delta,x:{\oplus}A}

Appendix 0.B
An Alternative Eager Semantics for
{{\mathsf{s}\pi\mkern-2.0mu}^{+}\mkern-1.0mu}

Let us consider a variant of
{{\mathsf{s}\pi\mkern-2.0mu}^{+}\mkern-1.0mu}
in which syntax, typing, and structural congruence are as in
\lx@sectionsign

2
, but with an
eagerly committing
semantics.
The idea is simple: we fully commit to a non-deterministic choice once a prefix synchronizes.

{[{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\shortrightarrow}_{\text{Id}}]}~~\frac{}{(\bm{\nu}x)({\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{N}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\big[}[x\mathbin{\leftrightarrow}y]{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\big]}\mathbin{|}Q)\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\llparenthesis}\mkern 1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{N}}\mkern Figure 9:

Eager reduction semantics for
{{\mathsf{s}\pi\mkern-2.0mu}^{+}\mkern-1.0mu}
.

The eager reduction semantics, denoted
\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\longrightarrow}}
, is given in
Figure

9
.
This semantics implements the full commitment of non-deterministic choices by committing ND-contexts to D-contexts as follows:

Definition 7
.

The
commitment
of an ND-context
{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{N}}
, denoted
{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\llparenthesis}\mkern 1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{N}}\mkern 1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\rrparenthesis}
, is defined as follows:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\llparenthesis}\mkern 1.0mu[\cdot]\mkern 1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\rrparenthesis}

\displaystyle:=[\cdot]

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\llparenthesis}\mkern 1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{N}}\mathbin{|}P\mkern 1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\rrparenthesis}

\displaystyle:={\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\llparenthesis}\mkern 1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{N}}\mkern 1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\rrparenthesis}\mathbin{|}P

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\llparenthesis}\mkern 1.0mu(\bm{\nu}x)({\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{N}}\mathbin{|}P)\mkern 1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\rrparenthesis}

\displaystyle:=(\bm{\nu}x)({\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\llparenthesis}\mkern 1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{N}}\mkern 1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\rrparenthesis}\mathbin{|}P)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\llparenthesis}\mkern 1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{N}}\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}P\mkern 1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\rrparenthesis}

\displaystyle:={\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\llparenthesis}\mkern 1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{N}}\mkern 1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\rrparenthesis}

Proposition 1

For any ND-context
{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{N}}
, the context
{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\llparenthesis}\mkern 1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{N}}\mkern 1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\rrparenthesis}
is a D-context.

Just as
{{\mathsf{s}\pi\mkern-2.0mu}^{+}\mkern-1.0mu}
with lazy semantics,
{{\mathsf{s}\pi\mkern-2.0mu}^{+}\mkern-1.0mu}
with
\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\longrightarrow}}
satisfies type preservation and deadlock-freedom.
See
Section

0.E.1
for details.

Theorem 0.B.1

(Type Preservation: Eager Semantics)

If
P\vdash\Gamma
, then both
P\equiv Q
and
P\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\longrightarrow}}Q
(for any
S
) imply
Q\vdash\Gamma
.

Proof (Sketch).

If
P\equiv Q
, the thesis follows directly from
Theorem

2.1
.
If
P\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\longrightarrow}}Q
, we apply induction on the derivation of the reduction.
In each case, we show that the commitment of ND-contexts (
Definition

7
) preserves typing.
∎

Theorem 0.B.2

(Deadlock-freedom: Eager Semantics)

If
P\vdash\emptyset
and
P\not\equiv\bm{0}
, then there is
R
such that
P\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\longrightarrow}}R
.

Proof (Sketch).

First, we write
P
in such a way that we can access all its unblocked prefixes.
Then we inductively show that there must be at least one pair of such prefixes that are connected by a restriction.
Hence, these prefixes are duals and thus the process can reduce.
∎

\displaystyle\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin]{30.0}{$\mathbin{\longrightarrow}$}}
