Figure 10:
Example

7
: Reductions of
M
and of
{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{u}
under lazy and eager semantics. In
{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{u}
, we write ‘
\pi(X)
’ for the permutations of finite set
X
.

It is insightful to formally contrast our lazy and eager semantics.
We discuss two different ways.

Lazy vs Eager, Part I: Translating
\lambda_{\mathtt{C}}^{\ell}
.

One way of comparing
\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.38,0.61,0.27}\leadsto}}
and
\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\longrightarrow}}
is to establish the correctness of the translation

{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{-}
(
Figure

7
) but now in the eager case.
It turns out that the eager semantics leads to a
strictly weaker
form of correctness, whereby completeness and weak soundness (cf.
Definition

6
) hold up to a precongruence
\succeq_{\mkern-2.0mu\scalebox{.8}{$\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}$}}
instead of an equivalence (as in
Theorem

4.2
).

The precongruence
\succeq_{\mkern-2.0mu\scalebox{.8}{$\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}$}}
is defined as follows:

\frac{}{P\succeq_{\mkern-2.0mu\scalebox{.8}{$\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}$}}P}\frac{P_{i}\succeq_{\mkern-2.0mu\scalebox{.8}{$\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}$}}P^{\prime}_{i}\quad{\scriptstyle i\in\{1,2\}}}{P_{1}\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}P_{2}\succeq_{\mkern-2.0mu\scalebox{.8}{$\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}$}}P^{\prime}_{i}}\frac{P\succeq_{\mkern-2.0mu\scalebox{.8}{$\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}$}}P^{\prime}\quad Q\succeq_{\mkern-2.0mu\scalebox{.8}{$\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}$}}Q^{\prime}}{P\mathbin{|}Q\succeq_{\mkern-2.0mu\scalebox{.8}{$\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}$}}P^{\prime}\mathbin{|}Q^{\prime}}\frac{P\succeq_{\mkern-2.0mu\scalebox{.8}{$\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}$}}P^{\prime}}{(\bm{\nu}x)P\succeq_{\mkern-2.0mu\scalebox{.8}{$\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}$}}(\bm{\nu}x)P^{\prime}}

Intuitively,
P\succeq_{\mkern-2.0mu\scalebox{.8}{$\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}$}}Q
says that
P
has at least as many branches as
Q
.
Translation correctness up to
\succeq_{\mkern-2.0mu\scalebox{.8}{$\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}$}}
thus means that
\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\longrightarrow}}
is “too eager”, as it
prematurely commits
to branches.

\appendixref

*a:tight proves that the translation under the eager semantics satisfies such criteria.
Before discussing the corresponding completeness and soundness results, we present an example.

Example 7

To contrast commitment in eager and lazy semantics (and their effect on the translation’s correctness), recall from
Example

6
the term
M
(
3
):

M=\big(x_{1}\ \lbag x_{2}\ \lbag x_{3}\ \mathtt{1}\rbag\rbag\big){\langle\!|}\lbag\mathtt{fail}^{\emptyset},y,I\rbag/x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}{|\!\rangle}

Figure

10
recalls the three branching reductions from
M
to
N_{1}
,
N_{2}
and
N_{3}
.
It also depicts a side-by-side comparison of the reductions of
{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{u}
under the lazy (
\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.38,0.61,0.27}\leadsto}}
) and eager (
\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\longrightarrow}}
) semantics.
In the figure,
\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.38,0.61,0.27}\leadsto}}^{\ast}
and
\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\longrightarrow}}^{\ast}
denote the reflexive, transitive closures of
\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.38,0.61,0.27}\leadsto}}
and
\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\longrightarrow}}
, respectively.

Under
\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.38,0.61,0.27}\leadsto}}
there are three different reduction paths, each resulting directly in the translation of one of
N_{1},N_{2},N_{3}
: after the first choice, the following choices are preserved.
In contrast, under
\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\longrightarrow}}
there are six different reduction paths, each resulting in a process that relates to the translation of one of
N_{1},N_{2},N_{3}
through
\succeq_{\mkern-2.0mu\scalebox{.8}{$\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}$}}
: after the first choice for an item from the bag is made, the semantics commits to choices for the other items.
∎

The correctness properties induced by
\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\longrightarrow}}
are
loose
(rather than
tight
, as in
\lx@sectionsign

4
; cf.
Definition

6
):

Theorem 0.B.3

(Loose Completeness (Under
\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\longrightarrow}}
))

If
{N}\mathbin{\longrightarrow}{M}
for a well-formed closed
\lambda_{\mathtt{C}}
-term
N
, then there exists
Q
such that
{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}{N}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{u}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\longrightarrow}}^{*}Q
and
{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}{M}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{u}\succeq_{\mkern-2.0mu\scalebox{.8}{$\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}$}}Q
.

Proof (Sketch).

By induction on reductions.
See
\lx@sectionsign

0.H.1
for details.
∎

Theorem 0.B.4

(Loose Weak Soundness (Under
\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\longrightarrow}}
))

If
{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}{N}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{u}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\longrightarrow}}^{*}Q
for a well-formed closed
\lambda_{\mathtt{C}}
-term
N
, then there exist
{N}^{\prime}
and
Q^{\prime}
such that
(i)
{N}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\longrightarrow}}^{*}{N}^{\prime}

and
(ii)
Q\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\longrightarrow}}^{*}Q^{\prime}
with

{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}{N}^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{u}\succeq_{\mkern-2.0mu\scalebox{.8}{$\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}$}}Q^{\prime}
.

While
\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.38,0.61,0.27}\leadsto}}
reduces multiple branches of a choice in lockstep,
\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\longrightarrow}}
reduces only one branch and discards the rest.
Accordingly, weak soundness under
\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.38,0.61,0.27}\leadsto}}
(
Theorem

0.G.3
) relates a sequence of lazy reductions to a sequence of reductions in
\lambda_{\mathtt{C}}^{\ell}
.
In contrast,
Theorem

0.B.4
is weaker: it relates a sequence of eager reductions to a subset of branches, as some branches may have been eagerly discarded.
Hence, the proof of
Theorem

0.B.4
has the added complexity of showing that every branch that is eagerly discarded must also be precongruent to a source reduction. This makes it difficult to apply induction directly, as we do not know which branches have been discarded in
{{\mathsf{s}\pi\mkern-2.0mu}^{+}\mkern-1.0mu}
.

More in details, to prove
Theorem

0.B.4
, we first show that
\succeq_{\mkern-2.0mu\scalebox{.8}{$\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}$}}
is stable under reductions.
We need a way of denoting all possible reductions from a process.
We define
P\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\longrightarrow}}\{P_{i}\}_{i\in I}
, for a fixed (maximal) finite set
I=\{i\mid P\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\longrightarrow}}P_{i}\}
.
Similarly, we define
P\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\longrightarrow}}^{*}\{P_{i}\}_{i\in I}
inductively: if
P\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\longrightarrow}}^{*}\{P_{i}\}_{i\in I}
and
P_{i}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\longrightarrow}}\{P_{j}\}_{j\in J_{i}}
for each
i\in I
, then
P\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\longrightarrow}}^{*}\{P_{j}\}_{j\in J}
with
J={\mathchoice{\textstyle}{}{}{}\bigcup}_{i\in I}J_{i}
.
We then have the following:

Proposition 2

If
P\succeq_{\mkern-2.0mu\scalebox{.8}{$\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}$}}Q
and
P\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\longrightarrow}}^{*}\{P_{i}\}_{i\in I}
,
then there exist
J
and
\{Q_{j}\}_{j\in J}
such that
Q\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\longrightarrow}}^{*}\{Q_{j}\}_{j\in J}
,
J\subseteq I
, and for each
j\in J
,
P_{j}\succeq_{\mkern-2.0mu\scalebox{.8}{$\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}$}}Q_{j}
.

Proof (Sketch).

By induction on the derivation rules of the precongruence
\succeq_{\mkern-2.0mu\scalebox{.8}{$\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}$}}
.
See
\lx@sectionsign

0.H.2
for details.
∎

Then, using
Proposition

2
we prove the following:

Lemma 1

Let
{N}
be a well-formed closed term.
If
{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}{N}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{u}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\longrightarrow}}^{*}Q
, then there exist
{N}^{\prime}
and
\{Q_{i}\}_{i\in I}
such that
(i)
{N}\mathbin{\longrightarrow}^{*}{N}^{\prime}

and
(ii)
Q\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\longrightarrow}}^{*}\{Q_{i}\}_{i\in I}
where for each
j\in I
,
{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}{N}^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{u}\succeq_{\mkern-2.0mu\scalebox{.8}{$\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}$}}Q_{j}
.

Lemma

1
ensures that the translation does not add behaviors not present in the source term.
Theorem

0.B.4
then follows directly from
Lemma

1
by taking an arbitrary
Q_{i}
.

Lazy vs Eager, Part II: Behavioral Equivalence.

One may ask if the differences between
\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.38,0.61,0.27}\leadsto}}
and
\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\longrightarrow}}
are confined to the ability to correctly translate
\lambda_{\mathtt{C}}^{\ell}
, or, relatedly, whether
\lambda_{\mathtt{C}}^{\ell}
’s formulation is responsible for these differences.

We now compare
\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.38,0.61,0.27}\leadsto}}
and
\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\longrightarrow}}

independently from
\lambda_{\mathtt{C}}^{\ell}

by resorting to
behavioral equivalences
.
We define a simple behavioral notion of equivalence on
{{\mathsf{s}\pi\mkern-2.0mu}^{+}\mkern-1.0mu}
processes, parametric in
\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.38,0.61,0.27}\leadsto}}
or
\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\longrightarrow}}
; then, we prove that there are classes of processes that are equal with respect to
\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.38,0.61,0.27}\leadsto}}
, but incomparable with respect to
\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\longrightarrow}}
(
Theorem

0.B.5
).
A key ingredient is the following notion of observable on processes:

Definition 8
.

A process
P
has a
ready-prefix

\alpha
, denoted
P\downarrow_{\alpha}
, if and only if there exist
{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{N}},P^{\prime}
such that
P\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{N}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}[}\alpha;P^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}]}
.

We may now define:

Definition 9

(Ready-Prefix Bisimilarity)
.

A relation
\mathbb{B}
on
{{\mathsf{s}\pi\mkern-2.0mu}^{+}\mkern-1.0mu}
processes is a
(strong) ready-prefix bisimulation with respect to
\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.38,0.61,0.27}\leadsto}}

if and only if, for every
(P,Q)\in\mathbb{B}
,

1.

For every
P^{\prime}
such that
P\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.38,0.61,0.27}\leadsto}}P^{\prime}
, there exists
Q^{\prime}
such that
Q\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.38,0.61,0.27}\leadsto}}Q^{\prime}
and
(P^{\prime},Q^{\prime})\in\mathbb{B}
;

2.

For every
Q^{\prime}
such that
Q\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.38,0.61,0.27}\leadsto}}Q^{\prime}
, there exists
P^{\prime}
such that
P\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.38,0.61,0.27}\leadsto}}P^{\prime}
and
(P^{\prime},Q^{\prime})\in\mathbb{B}
;

3.

For every
\alpha\bowtie\beta
,
P\downarrow_{\alpha}
if and only if
Q\downarrow_{\beta}
.

P
and
Q
are
ready-prefix bisimilar with respect to
\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.38,0.61,0.27}\leadsto}}

, denoted
P\sim_{\mkern-1.0mu\mathtt{L}}Q
, if there exists a relation
\mathbb{B}
that is a
ready-prefix bisimulation with respect to
\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.38,0.61,0.27}\leadsto}}
such that
(P,Q)\in\mathbb{B}
.

A
(strong) ready-prefix bisimulation with respect to
\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\longrightarrow}}

is defined by replacing every occurrence of ‘
\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.38,0.61,0.27}\leadsto}}
’ by ‘
\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\longrightarrow}}
’ in the definition above.
We write
P\sim_{\mkern-1.0mu\mathtt{E}}Q
if
P
and
Q
are
ready-prefix bisimilar with respect to
\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\longrightarrow}}

.

Ready-prefix bisimulation can highlight a subtle but significant difference between the behavior induced by our lazy and eager semantics.
To demonstrate this, we consider session-typed implementations of a vending machine.

Example 8

(Two Vending Machines)

Consider vending machines
\mathsf{VM}_{1}
and
\mathsf{VM}_{2}
consisting of three parts:
(1) an interface, which interacts with the user to send money and choose between coffee (
\mathsf{c}
) and tea (
\mathsf{t}
);
(2) a brewer, which produces either beverage;
(3) a system, which collects the money and forwards the user’s choice to the brewer.
A
{{\mathsf{s}\pi\mkern-2.0mu}^{+}\mkern-1.0mu}
specification follows (below
€
and
\textup{\texteuro}2
stand for names):

\displaystyle\mathsf{VM}_{1}

\displaystyle:=(\bm{\nu}x)\big(\mathsf{IF}_{1}\mathbin{|}(\bm{\nu}y)(\mathsf{Brewer}\mathbin{|}\mathsf{System})\big)

\displaystyle\mathsf{VM}_{2}

\displaystyle:=(\bm{\nu}x)\big(\mathsf{IF}_{2}\mathbin{|}(\bm{\nu}y)(\mathsf{Brewer}\mathbin{|}\mathsf{System})\big)

\displaystyle\mathsf{IF}_{1}

\displaystyle:=\overline{x}[\textup{\texteuro}2];\big(\overline{\textup{\texteuro}2}[]\mathbin{|}(\overline{x}.\mathsf{c};\overline{x}[]\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}\overline{x}.\mathsf{t};\overline{x}[])\big)

\displaystyle\mathsf{IF}_{2}

\displaystyle:=\overline{x}[\textup{\texteuro}2];(\overline{\textup{\texteuro}2}[]\mathbin{|}\overline{x}.\mathsf{c};\overline{x}[])\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}\overline{x}[\textup{\texteuro}2];(\overline{\textup{\texteuro}2}[]\mathbin{|}\overline{x}.\mathsf{t};\overline{x}[])

\displaystyle\mathsf{System}

\displaystyle:=x(\!\textup{\texteuro});x.\mathtt{case}\!\left\{\!\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\mathsf{c}:\overline{y}.\mathsf{c};x();\textup{\texteuro}();\overline{y}[],\\
\mathsf{t}:\overline{y}.\mathsf{t};x();\textup{\texteuro}();\overline{y}[]\end{array}\!\right\}

\displaystyle\mathsf{Brewer}

\displaystyle:=y.\mathtt{case}\{\mathsf{c}:y();\mathsf{Brew}_{\mathsf{c}},\mathsf{t}:y();\mathsf{Brew}_{\mathsf{t}}\}

where
\mathsf{Brew}_{\mathsf{c}}\vdash\emptyset
,
\mathsf{Brew}_{\mathsf{t}}\vdash\emptyset
, such that
\mathsf{VM}_{1}\vdash\emptyset
,
\mathsf{VM}_{2}\vdash\emptyset
.

We give two implementations of the interface:

\mathsf{IF}_{1}
sends the money and then chooses coffee or tea;

\mathsf{IF}_{2}
chooses sending the money and then requesting coffee, or sending the money and then requesting tea.
Then,
\mathsf{IF}_{1}
and
\mathsf{IF}_{2}
result in two different vending machines,
\mathsf{VM}_{1}
and
\mathsf{VM}_{2}
.

We have
\mathsf{VM}_{1}\not\sim_{\mkern-1.0mu\mathtt{E}}\mathsf{VM}_{2}
: the eager semantics distinguishes between the implementations; e.g.,
\mathsf{IF}_{1}
has a single money slot, a button for coffee, and another button for tea, whereas
\mathsf{IF}_{2}
has two money slots, one for coffee, and another for tea.
In contrast, under the lazy semantics, these machines are indistinguishable:

\mathsf{VM}_{1}\sim_{\mkern-1.0mu\mathtt{L}}\mathsf{VM}_{2}
.
∎

Example

8
highlights a difference in behavior between
\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.38,0.61,0.27}\leadsto}}
and
\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\longrightarrow}}
when a moment of choice is subtly altered.
The following theorem captures this distinction (see
\lx@sectionsign

0.I
):

Theorem 0.B.5

Take
R\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{N}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}[}\alpha_{1};(P\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}Q){\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}]}\vdash\emptyset
and
S\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{N}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}[}\alpha_{2};P\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}\alpha_{3};Q{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}]}\vdash\emptyset
, where
\alpha_{1}\bowtie\alpha_{2}\bowtie\alpha_{3}
and
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}
require a continuation.
Suppose that
P\not\sim_{\mkern-1.0mu\mathtt{L}}Q
and
P\not\sim_{\mkern-1.0mu\mathtt{E}}Q
.
Then (i)
R\sim_{\mkern-1.0mu\mathtt{L}}S
but (ii)
R\not\sim_{\mkern-1.0mu\mathtt{E}}S
.

Processes (
4
) and (
5
) from
\lx@sectionsign

4
(Page
4
) provide another example of a change in the moment of choice, different from the one discussed above.
In (
4
), the choice depends on
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
.
In (
5
), the choice does not depend on
\alpha_{1}
or
\alpha_{2}
, but on choices made in the context in which the process resides.
Though the choice is subtly changed between (
4
) and (
5
), the impact is significant: these processes are not ready-prefix bisimilar, with respect to neither semantics.
This is because, under both lazy and eager semantics, in (
4
) the two branches evolve in lockstep, whereas in (
5
) they evolve independently.

Appendix 0.C
Beyond Linear Resources

Our results extend to the language
\lambda_{\mathtt{C}}
, an extension of
\lambda_{\mathtt{C}}^{\ell}
with a more general bag which includes unrestricted resources: resources that may be used zero or more times.

Figure

11
gives the syntax of
\lambda_{\mathtt{C}}
-terms, bags and contexts.

A key difference with
\lambda_{\mathtt{C}}^{\ell}
is that variables
x,y,z,\ldots
have linear and unrestricted occurrences.
Notation
x[\mathtt{l}]
denotes a
linear
occurrence of
x
; we often omit the annotation ‘
[\mathtt{l}]
’, and a sequence
\widetilde{x}
always involves linear occurrences.
Notation
x[i]
denotes an
unrestricted
occurrence of
x
, explicitly referencing the
i
-th element of an unrestricted (ordered) bag.
The structure of a bag is now split into a linear and an unrestricted component: as in
\lambda_{\mathtt{C}}^{\ell}
, linear resources in bags cannot be duplicated, but unrestricted resources are always duplicated when consumed.
The empty unrestricted bag is denoted
\mathtt{1}^{!}
.
Notation
U_{i}
denotes the singleton bag at the
i
-th position in
U
; if there is no
i
-th position in
U
, then
U_{i}
defaults to
\mathtt{1}^{!}
.
We use ‘
\star
’ to combine a linear and an unrestricted bag, and unrestricted bags are joined via the non-commutative ‘
\diamond
’.

To account for the explicit distinction between linear and unrestricted occurrences of variables, we now have two forms of explicit substitution:

•

M{\langle\!|}C/x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}{|\!\rangle}
, a linear substitution as in
\lambda_{\mathtt{C}}^{\ell}
;

•

M\llfloor U/x^{!}\rrceil
, an unrestricted substitution of an unrestricted bag
U
for an unrestricted variable
x^{!}
in
M
, with the assumption that
x^{!}
does not appear in another unrestricted substitution in
M
.

\displaystyle M,N,L::=~

\displaystyle x[*]

variable

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt{}\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]

sharing

\displaystyle\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}~

\displaystyle\lambda x.M

abstraction

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt{}\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}M\langle\!\langle\raisebox{1.93748pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle

intermediate substitution

\displaystyle\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}~

\displaystyle(M\ B)

application

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt{}\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}M{\langle\!|}C/x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}{|\!\rangle}

linear substitution

\displaystyle\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}~

\displaystyle\mathtt{fail}^{\tilde{x}}

failure

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt{}\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}M\llfloor U/x\rrceil

unrestricted substitution

\displaystyle[*]::=~

\displaystyle[\mathtt{l}]\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}[i]~~i\in\mathbb{N}

annotations

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt\hphantom{{}\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}{}}A,B::=~C\star U

bag

\displaystyle U,V::=~

\displaystyle\mathtt{1}^{!}\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}{\lbag M\rbag^{!}}\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}{U\diamond V}

unrestricted bag

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt\hphantom{{}\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}{}}C,D::=\mathtt{1}\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}\lbag M\rbag\cdot\,C

linear bag

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}::=~

\displaystyle[\cdot]\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}(\mathcal{C}\ B)\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}\mathcal{C}{\langle\!|}C/\widetilde{x}{|\!\rangle}\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}\mathcal{C}\llfloor U/x\rrceil\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}\mathcal{C}[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]

context

Figure 11:
Syntax of
\lambda_{\mathtt{C}}
.

The reduction rules for
\lambda_{\mathtt{C}}
, extend the rules given in
Figure

5
with some modifications to accomodate the two-component format of bags and dedicated rules for the new constructs. Here, we describe the most interesting new rules: Rule
\mathtt{[RS{:}Ex\text{-}Sub]}
for explicit substitution, and also Rules
\mathtt{[R:Fetch^{!}]}
and
\mathtt{[R:Fail^{!}]}
for unrestricted substitution.

\frac{\mathsf{size}(C)=|\widetilde{x}|\quad M\not=\mathtt{fail}^{\tilde{y}}}{(M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x])\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$C\star U$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle\mathbin{\longrightarrow}M{\langle\!|}C/\widetilde{x}{|\!\rangle}\llfloor U/x\rrceil}\quad\textsc{$\mathtt{[RS{:}Ex\text{-}Sub]}$}\frac{\mathsf{head}(M)={x}[i]\quad U_{i}=\lbag N\rbag^{!}}{M\llfloor U/x\rrceil\mathbin{\longrightarrow}M\{N/{x}[i]\}\llfloor U/x\rrceil}\quad\textsc{$\mathtt{[RS{:}Fetch^{!}]}$}\frac{\mathsf{head}(M)={x}[i]\quad U_{i}=\mathtt{1}^{!}}{M\llfloor U/x\rrceil\mathbin{\longrightarrow}M\{\mathtt{fail}^{\emptyset}/{x}[i]\}\llfloor U/x\rrceil}\quad\textsc{$\mathtt{[RS{:}Fail^{!}]}$}

An explicit substitution
(M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x])\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$C\star U$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle
reduces to a term in which the linear and unrestricted parts of the bag are separated into their own explicit substitutions
M{\langle\!|}C/\widetilde{x}{|\!\rangle}\llfloor U/x\rrceil
, if successful. The fetching of linear/unrestricted resources from their corresponding bags is done by the appropriated fetch rules.
The reduction of an unrestricted substitution
M\llfloor U/x\rrceil
, where the head variable of
M
is
x[i]
, depends on
U_{i}
:

•

If
U_{i}=\lbag N\rbag^{!}
, then the term reduces via Rule
\mathtt{[R:Fetch^{!}]}
by substituting the head occurrence of
x[i]
in
M
with
N
, denoted
M\{N/x[i]\}
; note that
U_{i}
remains available after this reduction.

•

If
U_{i}=\mathtt{1}^{!}
, the head variable is instead substituted with failure via Rule
\mathtt{[R:Fail^{!}]}
.

The definition of
\mathsf{head}(M)
is as in
Figure

5
(bottom), extended with
\mathsf{head}(x[i])=x[i]
and
\mathsf{head}(M\llfloor U/x\rrceil)=\mathsf{head}(M)
. The complete set of rules is in
\appendixref
*ss:lambdaUnresBags.

Well-typedness and well-formedness.

Types for
\lambda_{\mathtt{C}}
extend the types for
\lambda_{\mathtt{C}}^{\ell}
in
Section

3.2
with:

\displaystyle\sigma,\tau,\delta::=~

\displaystyle\mathbf{unit}\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}(\pi,\eta)\rightarrow\sigma

\displaystyle\eta,\epsilon

\displaystyle::=\sigma\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}\epsilon\diamond\eta\qquad\text{list}

\displaystyle(\pi,\eta)\qquad\text{tuple}

The list type
\epsilon\diamond\eta
types the concatenation of unrestricted bags.
It can be recursively unfolded into a finite composition of strict types
\sigma_{1}\diamond\ldots\diamond\sigma_{n}
, for some
n\geq 1
, with length
n
and
\sigma_{i}
its
i
-th strict type (
1\leq i\leq n
).
We write
x^{!}:\eta
to denote for
x[1]:\eta_{1},\ldots,x[k]:\eta_{k}
where
\eta
has length
k
.
The tuple type
(\pi,\eta)
types concatenation of a linear bag of type
\pi
with an unrestricted bag of type
\eta
. Finally strict types are amended to allow for unrestricted functional types which go from tuple types to strict types
(\pi,\eta)\rightarrow\sigma
rather then multiset types to strict types.

We separate contexts into two parts: linear (
\Gamma,\Delta,\ldots
) and unrestricted (
\Theta,\Upsilon,\ldots
):

\displaystyle\Gamma,\Delta

\displaystyle::=\text{-}\ |\ \Gamma,x:\pi\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}\Gamma,x:\sigma

\displaystyle\Theta,\Upsilon

\displaystyle::=\text{-}\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}\Theta,x^{!}:\eta

Both linear and unrestricted occurrences of variables may occur at most once in a context.
Judgments have the form
\Theta;\Gamma\vDash M:\tau
.
We write
\vDash M:\tau
to denote
\text{-};\text{-}\vDash M:\tau
.

Well-formedness rules for
\lambda_{\mathtt{C}}
extend the rules given in
Figure

6
with specific rules to handle unrestricted resources, among those, we select Rules
\mathtt{[FS:var^{!}]}
,
\mathtt{[FS{:}abs\text{-}sh]}
,
\mathtt{[FS{:}Esub^{!}]}
and

\mathtt{[FS{:}bag^{!}]}
, for typing unrestricted occurrences of a variable, abstraction of a sharing term, explicit substitution and unrestricted bags, respectively.

\frac{\Theta,x^{!}:\eta;{x}:\eta_{i},\Delta\vDash{x}:\sigma}{\Theta,x^{!}:\eta;\Delta\vDash{x}[i]:\sigma}\quad\textsc{$\mathtt{[FS{:}var^{!}]}$}\frac{\Theta,x^{!}:\eta;\Gamma,{x}:\sigma^{k}\vDash M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]:\tau\quad{x}\notin\mathit{dom}(\Gamma)}{\Theta;\Gamma\vDash\lambda x.(M[{\widetilde{x}}\leftarrow{x}]):(\sigma^{k},\eta)\rightarrow\tau}\quad\textsc{$\mathtt{[FS{:}abs\text{-}sh]}$}\frac{\Theta,x^{!}{:}\eta;\Gamma\vDash M:\tau\quad\Theta;\text{-}\vDash U:\epsilon\quad\eta\sim\epsilon}{\Theta;\Gamma\vDash M\llfloor U/x^{!}\rrceil:\tau}\quad\textsc{$\mathtt{[FS{:}Esub^{!}]}$}\frac{\Theta;\text{-}\vDash U:\epsilon\quad\Theta;\text{-}\vDash V:\eta}{\Theta;\text{-}\vDash U\diamond V:\epsilon\diamond\eta}\quad\textsc{$\mathtt{[FS{:}bag^{!}]}$}

In Rule
\mathtt{[FS{:}Esub!]}
,
\eta\sim\epsilon
denotes the fact that
\epsilon=\epsilon_{1}\diamond\ldots\diamond\epsilon_{k}\diamond\ldots\diamond\epsilon_{n}
and
\eta=\eta_{1}\diamond\ldots\diamond\eta_{k}
are two list types, with
n\geq k
, such that the following hold: for all
i
,
1\leq i\leq k
,
\epsilon_{i}=\eta_{i}
. The complete set of well-formedness rules for
\lambda_{\mathtt{C}}
is in
\appendixref
*ss:lambdaUnresBags.

Extended translation.

To extend the translation
in Fig.
7
to
\lambda_{\mathtt{C}}
:
The access and use of unrestricted resources in
\lambda_{\mathtt{C}}
is codified in
{{\mathsf{s}\pi\mkern-2.0mu}^{+}\mkern-1.0mu}
by combining labeled choices and clients/servers (
\appendixref
*ss:transUnres).

Note: for the sake of generality the proofs in the appendices concern
\lambda_{\mathtt{C}}
.

Figure

12
defines the free linear variables of a term, denoted
\mathsf{lfv}(M)
.

Figure

13
gives the reduction rules for
\lambda_{\mathtt{C}}
, extending the rules given in
Figure

5
with Rules
\mathtt{[R:Fetch^{!}]}
and
\mathtt{[R:Fail^{!}]}
for unrestricted substitution.

\displaystyle\mathsf{lfv}(x)

\displaystyle=\{x\}

\displaystyle\mathsf{lfv}((M\ B))

\displaystyle=\mathsf{lfv}(M)\cup\mathsf{lfv}(B)

\displaystyle\mathsf{lfv}(x[i])

\displaystyle=\emptyset

\displaystyle\mathsf{lfv}(M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x])

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{lfv}(M)\setminus\{\widetilde{x}\})\cup\{x\}

\displaystyle\mathsf{lfv}(\mathtt{1})

\displaystyle=\emptyset

\displaystyle\mathsf{lfv}(\lambda x.(M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]))

\displaystyle=\mathsf{lfv}(M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x])\setminus\{x\}

\displaystyle\mathsf{lfv}(\lbag M\rbag)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{lfv}(M)

\displaystyle\mathsf{lfv}(M{\langle\!|}C/x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}{|\!\rangle})

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{lfv}(M)\setminus\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\})\cup\mathsf{lfv}(C)

\displaystyle\mathsf{lfv}(C\star U)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{lfv}(C)

\displaystyle\mathsf{lfv}(M\langle\!\langle\raisebox{1.93748pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle)

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{lfv}(M)\setminus\{x\})\cup\mathsf{lfv}(B)

\displaystyle\mathsf{lfv}(\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{x}})

\displaystyle=\{\widetilde{x}\}

\displaystyle\mathsf{lfv}(\lbag M\rbag\cdot C)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{lfv}(M)\cup\mathsf{lfv}(C)

In the case
\mathsf{lfv}(M)=\emptyset
, the term
M
is called
linearly closed
.

\displaystyle::=\sigma\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}\epsilon\diamond\eta\qquad\text{list}

\displaystyle(\pi,\eta)\qquad\text{tuple}

The list type
\epsilon\diamond\eta
types the concatenation of unrestricted bags.
It can be recursively unfolded into a finite composition of strict types
\sigma_{1}\diamond\ldots\diamond\sigma_{n}
, for some
n\geq 1
, with length

and
\sigma_{i}

Definition 10

(
\eta\sim\epsilon
)
.

Let
\epsilon
and
\eta
be two list types, with the length of
\epsilon
greater or equal to that of
\eta
.
We say that
\epsilon

embraces

\eta
, denoted
\eta\sim\epsilon
, whenever there exist
\epsilon^{\prime}
and
\epsilon^{\prime\prime}
such that: i)
\epsilon=\epsilon^{\prime}\diamond\epsilon^{\prime\prime}
; ii) the size of
\epsilon^{\prime}
is that of
\eta
; iii) for all
i
,
\epsilon^{\prime}_{i}=\eta_{i}
.

We separate contexts into two parts: linear (
\Gamma,\Delta,\ldots
) and unrestricted (
\Theta,\Upsilon,\ldots
):

\displaystyle\Gamma,\Delta

\displaystyle::=\text{-}\ |\ \Gamma,x:\pi\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}\Gamma,x:\sigma

\displaystyle\Theta,\Upsilon

\displaystyle::=\text{-}\mathbin{\scalebox{1.3}{$\mid$}}\Theta,x^{!}:\eta

Both linear and unrestricted occurrences of variables may occur at most once in a context.

Judgments have the form
\Theta;\Gamma\vDash M:\tau
.
We write
\vDash M:\tau
to denote
\text{-};\text{-}\vDash M:\tau
.

Definition 11

(Well-formedness in
\lambda_{\mathtt{C}}
)
.

A
\lambda_{\mathtt{C}}
-term
M
is
well-formed
if there exists a context
\Theta
and
\Gamma
and a type
\tau
such that the rules in
Figure

14
entail
\Theta;\Gamma\vDash M:\tau
.

\frac{}{\Theta;{x}:\sigma\vDash{x}:\sigma}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{$\mathtt{[FS{:}var^{!}]}$}\frac{}{\Theta;\text{-}\vDash\mathtt{1}:\omega}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{$\mathtt{[FS{:}\mathtt{1}^{\ell}]}$}\frac{\Theta;\Gamma\vDash M:\sigma\hskip 9.24994pt\Theta;\text{-}\vDash V:\eta}{\Theta;\text{-}\vDash U\diamond V:\epsilon\diamond\eta}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{$\mathtt{[FS{:}bag^{!}]}$}\frac{\Theta;\Gamma\vDash C:\sigma^{k}\hskip A congruence.

Some terms, though syntactically different, display the same behavior.
For example, assuming
x\notin\mathsf{fv}(M)
, this holds for
M\llfloor U/x^{!}\rrceil
and
M
: the former describes a substitution that “does nothing” and would result in
M
itself.
This notion is formalized through a
congruence
(
\equiv_{\lambda}
) closed under syntax, given in
Figure

15
.

\frac{\widetilde{x}\mathbin{\#}\mathsf{fv}(B)}{(M\ B){\langle\!|}C/\widetilde{x}{|\!\rangle}\equiv_{\lambda}(M{\langle\!|}C/\widetilde{x}{|\!\rangle})\ B}\frac{x\not\in\mathsf{fv}(M)}{M\llfloor U/x^{!}\rrceil\equiv_{\lambda}M}\frac{x\not\in\mathsf{fv}(B)}{(M\ B)\llfloor U/x^{!}\rrceil\equiv_{\lambda}(M\llfloor U/x^{!}\rrceil)\ B}\frac{\widetilde{x}\mathbin{\#}\mathsf{fv}(A)}{\big((M\ A)[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]\big)\langle\!\langle\raisebox{1.93748pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle\equiv_{\lambda}\big((M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x])\langle\!\langle\raisebox{1.93748pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle\big)\ A}\frac{\widetilde{x}\mathbin{\#}\mathsf{fv}(A)\hskip 9.24994pt\widetilde{y}\mathbin{\#}\mathsf{fv}(B)}{\Big(\big((M[\widetilde{y}\leftarrow y])\langle\!\langle\raisebox{1.93748pt}{\small$A$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\rangle\!\rangle\big)[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]\Big)\langle\!\langle\raisebox{1.93748pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle\equiv_{\lambda}\Big(\big(M[\widetilde{y}\leftarrow y])\langle\!\langle\raisebox{1.93748pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\rangle\!\rangle\big)[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]\Big)\langle\!\langle\raisebox{1.93748pt}{\small$A$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle}\frac{\widetilde{x}\mathbin{\#}\mathsf{fv}(B)\hskip 9.24994pt\widetilde{y}\mathbin{\#}\mathsf{fv}(C)}{\big((M[\widetilde{y}\leftarrow y])\langle\!\langle\raisebox{1.93748pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\rangle\!\rangle\big){\langle\!|}C/\widetilde{x}{|\!\rangle}\equiv_{\lambda}\big((M{\langle\!|}C/\widetilde{x}{|\!\rangle})[\widetilde{y}\leftarrow y]\big)\langle\!\langle\raisebox{1.93748pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\rangle\!\rangle}\frac{x\not\in\mathsf{fv}(B)\hskip 9.24994pt\widetilde{y}\mathbin{\#}\mathsf{fv}(U)}{\big((M[\widetilde{y}\leftarrow y])\langle\!\langle\raisebox{1.93748pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\rangle\!\rangle\big)\llfloor 9.24994pty\not\in\mathsf{fv}(C)}{(M{\langle\!|}C/\widetilde{x}{|\!\rangle})\llfloor U/y^{!}\rrceil\equiv_{\lambda}(M\llfloor U/y^{!}\rrceil){\langle\!|}C/\widetilde{x}{|\!\rangle}}\frac{x\not\in\mathsf{fv}(U)\hskip 9.24994pty\not\in\mathsf{fv}(V)}{(M\llfloor V/\widetilde{x}\rrceil)\llfloor .

0.D.2
Translating
\lambda_{\mathtt{C}}
into (Full)
{{\mathsf{s}\pi\mkern-2.0mu}^{+}\mkern-1.0mu}

Figure

7
gives the translation of
\lambda_{\mathtt{C}}^{\ell}
into
{{\mathsf{s}\pi\mkern-2.0mu}^{+}\mkern-1.0mu}
.
Here we extend this translation to consider the extended calculus
\lambda_{\mathtt{C}}
.
The key differences are in the translation of unrestricted variable occurrences, intermediate substitution, abstraction, and the new structure of bags.

Figure

16
gives the translation that maps terms in
\lambda_{\mathtt{C}}
into processes in full
{{\mathsf{s}\pi\mkern-2.0mu}^{+}\mkern-1.0mu}
, denoted
{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{u}
.

The translation of an unrestricted variable
x[j]
first connects to a server along channel
x
via a request
{?}\overline{x^{!}}[{x_{i}}]
followed by a selection on
\overline{x_{i}}.j
.

Process
{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}\lambda x.(M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]){\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{u}
first confirms its behavior along
u
, followed by the reception of a channel
x
.
The channel
x
provides a linear channel
x^{\ell}
and an unrestricted channel
x^{!}
for dedicated substitutions of the linear and unrestricted bag components.
This separation is also present in the translation of
{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}M\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{u}
, for the same reason.

Process
{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}M\,(C\star U)_{u}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}
consists of synchronizations between the translation of
{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{v}
and

{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}C\star U{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{x}
: the translation
of
C\star U
evolves when
M
is an abstraction, say

{\lambda x.(M^{\prime}[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x])}
.
The channel
x^{\ell}
provides the linear behavior of the bag
C
while
x^{!}
provides the behavior of
U
; this is done by guarding the translation of
U
with a server connection, such that every time a channel synchronizes with it a fresh copy of
U
is spawned.

Process
{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}M\llfloor U/x^{!}\rrceil{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{u}
consists of the composition of the translation of
M
and a server guarding the translation of
U
: in order for
{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{u}
to gain access to
{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}U{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{x_{i}}
it must first synchronize with the server channel
x^{!}
to spawn a fresh copy of the translation of
U
.

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}{x}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{u}

\displaystyle=\overline{x}.\mathtt{some};[x\mathbin{\leftrightarrow}u]\hskip 28.45274pt{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}{x}[j]{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{u}={?}\overline{x^{!}}[{x_{i}}];\overline{x_{i}}.j;[x_{i}\mathbin{\leftrightarrow}u]

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}\lambda x.M{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{u}

\displaystyle=\overline{u}.\mathtt{some};u(x);\overline{x}.\mathtt{some};x(x^{\ell});x(x^{!});x();{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{u}

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}M\langle\!\langle\raisebox{1.93748pt}{\small$C\star U$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{u}

\displaystyle=(\bm{\nu}x)(\overline{x}.\mathtt{some};x(x^{\ell});x(x^{!});x();{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{u}\mathbin{|}{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}C\star U{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{x})

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}M(C\star U){\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{u}

\displaystyle=(\bm{\nu}v)({{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{v}\mathbin{|}{v.\mathtt{some}}_{u,\mathsf{lfv}(C)};\overline{v}[x];({{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}C\star U{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{x}\mathbin{|}[v\mathbin{\leftrightarrow}u]))

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}C\star U{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{x}

\displaystyle={x.\mathtt{some}}_{\mathsf{lfv}(C)};\overline{x}[x^{\ell}];\big({{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}C{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{x^{\ell}}\mathbin{|}\overline{x}[x^{!}];({!}x^{!}(x_{i});{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}U{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{x_{i}}\mathbin{|}\overline{x}[])\big)

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}\lbag M_{j}\rbag\cdot~C{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{x^{\ell}}

\displaystyle=\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}{x^{\ell}.\mathtt{some}}_{\mathsf{lfv}(C)};x(y_{i});{x^{\ell}.\mathtt{some}}_{y_{i},\mathsf{lfv}(C)};\overline{x^{\ell}}.\mathtt{some};\\
\hskip 9.24994pt\overline{x^{\ell}}[z_{i}];({z_{i}.\mathtt{some}}_{\mathsf{lfv}(M_{j})};{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}M_{j}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{z_{i}}\mathbin{|}({{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}(C\setminus M_{j}){\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{x^{\ell}}\mathbin{|}\overline{y_{i}}.\mathtt{none}))\end{array}

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}{\mathtt{1}}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{x^{\ell}}

\displaystyle={x^{\ell}.\mathtt{some}}_{\emptyset};x(y_{n});(\overline{y_{n}}.\mathtt{some};\overline{y_{n}}[]\mathbin{|}{x^{\ell}.\mathtt{some}}_{\emptyset};\overline{x^{\ell}}.\mathtt{none})

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}\mathtt{1}^{!}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{x}=\overline{x}.\mathtt{none}\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}\lbag N\rbag^{!}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{x}={{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}N{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{x}\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}U{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{x}=x.\mathtt{case}\{i:{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}U_{i}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{x}\}_{U_{i}\in U}

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\left\llbracket M{\langle\!|}\lbag M_{1}\rbag\cdot\lbag M_{2}\rbag{}/x_{1},x_{2}{|\!\rangle}\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\right\rrbracket_{u}

\displaystyle=\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}(\bm{\nu}z_{1})({z_{1}.\mathtt{some}}_{\mathsf{lfv}(M_{1})};{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}M_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{{z_{1}}}\mathbin{|}(\bm{\nu}z_{2})({z_{2}.\mathtt{some}}_{\mathsf{lfv}(M_{2})};{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}M_{2}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{{z_{2}}}\\

\displaystyle=\overline{u}.\mathtt{none}\mathbin{|}\overline{x_{1}}.\mathtt{none}\mathbin{|}\ldots\mathbin{|}\overline{x_{k}}.\mathtt{none}

Definition 12

The translation
{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{\_}
in

extends to a linear context

\Gamma={{x}_{1}:\sigma_{1}},\ldots,{{x}_{m}:\sigma_{m}},{{v}_{1}:\pi_{1}},\ldots,{v}_{n}:\pi_{n}

and an unrestricted context
\Theta=x^{!}[1]:\eta_{1},\ldots,x^{!}[k]:\eta_{k}
as follows:

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}\Gamma{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}

\displaystyle={\binampersand}\mathbf{1}

\displaystyle={\binampersand}(\overline{{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}(\sigma^{k},\eta){\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}}_{(\sigma,i)}}\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\llbracket}\tau{\color[rgb]{0.85,0,0.42}\rrbracket}})

Appendix 0.E
Proofs of Type Preservation and Deadlock-freedom for (Full)
{{\mathsf{s}\pi\mkern-2.0mu}^{+}\mkern-1.0mu}

Here we prove
Theorems

2.1
and
2.2
(type preservation and deadlock-freedom for the lazy semantics, respectively), as well as the analogue results for the eager semantics.
In fact, deadlock-freedom for the lazy semantics follows from deadlock-freedom for the eager semantics, so we present the proofs for the eager semantics first.

0.E.1
Eager Semantics

Subject Congruence

Theorem 0.E.1

If
P\vdash\Gamma
and
P\equiv Q
, then
Q\vdash\Gamma
.

Proof.

By induction on the derivation of the structural congruence.
We first detail the base cases:

•

P\equiv_{\alpha}P^{\prime}\implies P\equiv P^{\prime}
.
Since alpha-renaming only affects bound names, it does not affect the names in
\Gamma
, so clearly
P^{\prime}\vdash\Gamma
.

•

[x\mathbin{\leftrightarrow}y]\equiv[y\mathbin{\leftrightarrow}x]
.

\frac{}{[x\mathbin{\leftrightarrow}y]\vdash x{:}A,y{:}\overline{A}}\equiv\frac{}{[y\mathbin{\leftrightarrow}x]\vdash x{:}A,y{:}\overline{A}}

•

P\mathbin{|}Q\equiv Q\mathbin{|}P
.

\frac{P\vdash\Gamma\quad Q\vdash\Delta}{P\mathbin{|}Q\vdash\Gamma,\Delta}\equiv\frac{Q\vdash\Delta\quad P\vdash\Gamma}{Q\mathbin{|}P\vdash\Gamma,\Delta}

•

(P\mathbin{|}Q)\mathbin{|}R\equiv P\mathbin{|}(Q\mathbin{|}R)
.

\frac{\frac{P\vdash\Gamma\hskip 8.19447ptQ\vdash\Delta}{P\mathbin{|}Q\vdash\Gamma,\Delta}\quad R\vdash\Lambda}{(P\mathbin{|}Q)\mathbin{|}R\vdash\Gamma,\Delta,\Lambda}\equiv\frac{P\vdash\Gamma\quad\frac{Q\vdash\Delta\hskip 8.19447ptR\vdash\Lambda}{Q\mathbin{|}R\vdash\Delta,\Lambda}}{P\mathbin{|}(Q\mathbin{|}R)\vdash\Gamma,\Delta,\Lambda}

•

P\mathbin{|}\bm{0}\equiv P

\frac{P\vdash\Gamma\quad\frac{}{\bm{0}\vdash\emptyset}}{P\mathbin{|}\bm{0}\vdash\Gamma}\equiv P\vdash\Gamma

•

(\bm{\nu}x)(P\mathbin{|}Q)\equiv(\bm{\nu}x)(Q\mathbin{|}P)
.

\frac{P\vdash\Gamma,x{:}A\quad Q\vdash\Delta,x{:}\overline{A}}{(\bm{\nu}x)(P\mathbin{|}Q)\vdash\Gamma,\Delta}\equiv\frac{Q\vdash\Delta,x{:}\overline{A}\quad P\vdash\Gamma,x{:}A}{(\bm{\nu}x)(Q\mathbin{|}P)\vdash\Gamma,\Delta}

•

x\notin\mathit{fn}(Q)\implies(\bm{\nu}x)((\bm{\nu}y)(P\mathbin{|}Q)\mathbin{|}R)\equiv(\bm{\nu}y)((\bm{\nu}x)(P\mathbin{|}R)\mathbin{|}Q)
.

\frac{\frac{P\vdash\Gamma,y{:}A,x{:}B\hskip 8.19447ptQ\vdash\Delta,y{:}\overline{A}}{(\bm{\nu}y)(P\mathbin{|}Q)\vdash\Gamma,\Delta,x{:}B}\quad R\vdash\Lambda,x{:}\overline{B}}{(\bm{\nu}x)((\bm{\nu}y)(P\mathbin{|}Q)\mathbin{|}R)\vdash\Gamma,\Delta,\Lambda}\equiv\frac{\frac{P\vdash\Gamma,y{:}A,x{:}B\hskip 8.19447ptR\vdash\Lambda,x{:}\overline{B}}{(\bm{\nu}x)(P\mathbin{|}R)\vdash\Gamma,\Lambda,y{:}A}\quad Q\vdash\Delta,y{:}\overline{A}}{(\bm{\nu}y)((\bm{\nu}x)(P\mathbin{|}R)\mathbin{|}Q)\vdash\Gamma,\Delta,\Lambda}

•

x\notin\mathit{fn}(Q)\implies(\bm{\nu}x)((P\mathbin{|}Q)\mathbin{|}R)\equiv(\bm{\nu}x)(P\mathbin{|}R)\mathbin{|}Q
.

\frac{\frac{P\vdash\Gamma,x{:}A\hskip 8.19447ptQ\vdash\Delta}{P\mathbin{|}Q\vdash\Gamma,\Delta,x{:}A}\quad R\vdash\Lambda,x{:}\overline{A}}{(\bm{\nu}x)((P\mathbin{|}Q)\mathbin{|}R)\vdash\Gamma,\Delta,\Lambda}\equiv\frac{\frac{P\vdash\Gamma,x{:}A\hskip 8.19447ptR\vdash\Lambda,x{:}\overline{A}}{(\bm{\nu}x)(P\mathbin{|}R)\vdash\Gamma,\Lambda}\quad Q\vdash\Delta}{(\bm{\nu}x)(P\mathbin{|}R)\mathbin{|}Q\vdash\Gamma,\Delta,\Lambda}

•

x\notin\mathit{fn}(Q)\implies(\bm{\nu}x)({!}x(y);P\mathbin{|}Q)\equiv Q
.

\frac{\frac{P\vdash{?}\Gamma,y{:}A}{{!}x(y);P\vdash{?}\Gamma,x{:}{!}A}\quad\frac{Q\vdash\Delta}{Q\vdash\Delta,x{:}{?}\overline{A}}}{(\bm{\nu}x)({!}x(y);P\mathbin{|}Q)\vdash{?}\Gamma,\Delta}\equiv\frac{Q\vdash\Delta}{Q\vdash{?}\Gamma,\Delta}\quad\textsc{vcenter,fraction={===}}

•

P\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}Q\equiv Q\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}P
.

\frac{P\vdash\Gamma\quad Q\vdash\Gamma}{P\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}Q\vdash\Gamma}\equiv\frac{Q\vdash\Gamma\quad P\vdash\Gamma}{Q\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}P\vdash\Gamma}

•

(P\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}Q)\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}R\equiv P\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}(Q\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}R)

\frac{\frac{P\vdash\Gamma\hskip 8.19447ptQ\vdash\Gamma}{P\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}Q\vdash\Gamma}\quad R\vdash\Gamma}{(P\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}Q)\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}R\vdash\Gamma}\equiv\frac{P\vdash\Gamma\quad\frac{Q\vdash\Gamma\hskip 8.19447ptR\vdash\Gamma}{Q\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}R\vdash\Gamma}}{P\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}(Q\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}R)\vdash\Gamma}

•

P\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}P\equiv P
.

\frac{P\vdash\Gamma\quad P\vdash\Gamma}{P\mathbin{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\mkern-0.4mu{}\mathbin{|\mkern-3.0mu|}{}$\hss}$-$}}P\vdash\Gamma}\equiv P\vdash\Gamma

The inductive cases follow from the IH straightforwardly.
Note that the rules for parallel composition do not apply directly behind the output prefix and restriction.
∎

Subject Reduction

Lemma 2

Suppose
P\vdash\Gamma,x{:}A
.

1.

If
P={\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{N}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}[}\overline{x}[]{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}]}
, then
A=\bm{1}
.

2.

If
P={\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{N}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}[}x();P^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}]}
, then
A=\bot
.

3.

If
P={\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{N}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}[}\overline{x}[y];(P^{\prime}\mathbin{|}P^{\prime\prime}){\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}]}
, then
A=B\mathbin{\otimes}C
.

4.

If
P={\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{N}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}[}x(y);P^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}]}
, then
A=B\mathbin{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle{{\binampersand}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle{{\binampersand}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle{{\binampersand}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle{{\binampersand}}$}}}C
.

5.

If
P={\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{N}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}[}\overline{x}.j;P^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}]}
, then
A={\oplus}\{i:B_{i}\}_{i\in I}
where
j\in I
.

6.

If
P={\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{N}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}[}x.\mathtt{case}\{i:P^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i\in I}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}]}
, then
A={{\binampersand}}\{i:B_{i}\}_{i\in I}
.

7.

If
P={\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{N}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}[}\overline{x}.\mathtt{some};P^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}]}
, then
A={{\binampersand}}B
.

8.

If
P={\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{N}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}[}\overline{x}.\mathtt{none}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}]}
, then
A={{\binampersand}}B
.

9.

If
P={\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{N}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}[}{x.\mathtt{some}}_{w_{1},\ldots,w_{n}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}]}
, then
A={\oplus}B
.

10.

If
P={\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{N}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}[}{?}\overline{x}[y];P^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}]}
, then
A={?}B
.

11.

If
P={\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{N}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}[}{!}x(y);P^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}]}
, then
A={!}B
.

Proof.

Each item follows by induction on the structure of the ND-context.
The base case follows by inversion of typing, and the inductive cases follow from the IH straightforwardly.
∎

Lemma 3

For each of the following items, assume
\Gamma\mathbin{\#}\Delta
.

1.

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{N}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\big[}[x\mathbin{\leftrightarrow}y]{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\big]}\vdash\Gamma,x{:}A
and
Q\vdash\Delta,x{:}\overline{A}
, then
{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\llparenthesis}\mkern 1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{N}}\mkern 1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\rrparenthesis}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}[}Q\{y/x\}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}]}\vdash\Gamma,\Delta
.

2.

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{N}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}[}\overline{x}[]{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}]}\vdash\Gamma,x{:}\bm{1}
, then
{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\llparenthesis}\mkern 1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{N}}\mkern 1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\rrparenthesis}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}[}\bm{0}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}]}\vdash\Gamma
.

3.

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{N}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}[}x();Q{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}]}\vdash\Gamma,x{:}\bot
, then
{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\llparenthesis}\mkern 1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{N}}\mkern 1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\rrparenthesis}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}[}Q{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}]}\vdash\Gamma
.

4.

If
\mathit{bn}({\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{N}})\mathbin{\#}\mathit{fn}({\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{N}}^{\prime})
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{N}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}[}\overline{x}[y];(P\mathbin{|}Q){\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}]}\vdash\Gamma,x{:}A\mathbin{\otimes}B
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{N^{\prime}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}[}x(z);R{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}]}\vdash\Delta,x{:}\overline{A}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle{{\binampersand}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle{{\binampersand}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle{{\binampersand}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle{{\binampersand}}$}}}\overline{B}
, then
{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\llparenthesis}\mkern 1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{N}}\mkern 1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\rrparenthesis}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}[}(\bm{\nu}x)(Q\mathbin{|}(\bm{\nu}y)(P\mathbin{|}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\llparenthesis}\mkern 1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{N^{\prime}}}\mkern 1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\rrparenthesis}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}[}R\{y/z\}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}]})){\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}]}\vdash\Gamma,\Delta
.

5.

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{N}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}[}\overline{x}.j;P{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}]}\vdash\Gamma,x{:}{\oplus}\{i:A_{i}\}_{i\in I}
and
j\in I
, then
{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\llparenthesis}\mkern 1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{N}}\mkern 1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\rrparenthesis}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}[}P{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}]}\vdash\Gamma,x{:}A_{j}
.

6.

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{N}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}[}x.\mathtt{case}\{i:P_{i}\}_{i\in I}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}]}\vdash\Gamma,x{:}{\binampersand}\{i:A_{i}\}_{i\in I}
, then
{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\llparenthesis}\mkern 1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{N}}\mkern 1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\rrparenthesis}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}[}P_{i}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}]}\vdash\Gamma,x{:}A_{i}
for every
i\in I
.

7.

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{N}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}[}\overline{x}.\mathtt{some};P{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}]}\vdash\Gamma,x{:}{{\binampersand}}A
, then
{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\llparenthesis}\mkern 1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{N}}\mkern 1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\rrparenthesis}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}[}P{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}]}\vdash\Gamma,x{:}A
.

8.

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{N}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}[}\overline{x}.\mathtt{none}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}]}\vdash\Gamma,x{:}{{\binampersand}}A
, then
{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\llparenthesis}\mkern 1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{N}}\mkern 1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\rrparenthesis}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}[}\bm{0}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}]}\vdash\Gamma
.

9.

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{N}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}[}{x.\mathtt{some}}_{w_{1},\ldots,w_{n}};P{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}]}\vdash\Gamma,x{:}{\oplus}A
, then
{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\llparenthesis}\mkern 1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{N}}\mkern 1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\rrparenthesis}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}[}P{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}]}\vdash\Gamma,x{:}A
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\llparenthesis}\mkern 1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{N}}\mkern 1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\rrparenthesis}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}[}\overline{w_{1}}.\mathtt{none}\mathbin{|}\ldots\mathbin{|}\overline{w_{n}}.\mathtt{none}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}]}\vdash\Gamma
.

10.

If
\mathit{bn}({\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{N^{\prime}}})\mathbin{\#}\mathit{fn}({\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{N}})
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{N}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}[}{?}\overline{x}[y];P{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}]}\vdash\Gamma,x{:}{?}A
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{N}}^{\prime}[{!}x(y);Q]\vdash\Delta,x{:}{!}\overline{A}
, then
{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\llparenthesis}\mkern 1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{N^{\prime}}}\mkern 1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\rrparenthesis}\big[(\bm{\nu}x)((\bm{\nu}y)({\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\llparenthesis}\mkern 1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\mathtt{N}}\mkern 1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}\rrparenthesis}{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}[}P{\color[rgb]{0,0.56,0.61}]}\mathbin{|}Q\{y/z\})\mathbin{|}{!}x(z);Q)\big]
.

Proof.

For each item, we apply induction on the structure of the ND-contexts and detail the base cases.
For simplicity, we assume no names in
\Gamma
and
\Delta
were derived with
[
Tweaken
]
.

1.

\frac{}{[x\mathbin{\leftrightarrow}y]\vdash x{:}A,y{:}\overline{A}}\frac{}{Q\vdash\Delta,x{:}\overline{A}}\implies\frac{}{Q\{y/x\}\vdash\Delta,y{:}\overline{A}}

2.

\frac{}{\overline{x}[]\vdash x{:}\bm{1}}\implies\frac{}{\bm{0}\vdash\emptyset}

3.

\frac{Q\vdash\Gamma}{x();Q\vdash\Gamma,x{:}\bot}\implies\frac{}{Q\vdash\Gamma}

4.

\frac{P\vdash\Gamma,y{:}A\quad Q\vdash\Gamma^{\prime},x{:}B}{\overline{x}[y];(P\mathbin{|}Q)\vdash\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime},x{:}A\mathbin{\otimes}B}\frac{R\vdash\Delta,z{:}\overline{A},x{:}\overline{B}}{x(z);R\vdash\Delta,x{:}\overline{A}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle{{\binampersand}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle{{\binampersand}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle{{\binampersand}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle{{\binampersand}}$}}}\overline{B}}\implies\frac{Q\vdash\Gamma^{\prime},x{:}B\quad\frac{P\vdash\Gamma,y{:}A\hskip 8.19447ptR\{y/z\}\vdash\Delta,y{:}\overline{A},x{:}\overline{B}}{(\bm{\nu}y)(P\mathbin{|}R\{y/z\})\vdash\Gamma,\Delta,x{:}\overline{B}}}{(\bm{\nu}x)(Q\mathbin{|}(\bm{\nu}y)(P\mathbin{|}R\{y/z\}))\vdash\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime},\Delta}

5.

\frac{P\vdash\Gamma,x{:}A_{j}\quad j\in I}{\overline{x}.j;P\vdash\Gamma,x{:}{\oplus}\{i:A_{i}\}_{i\in I}}\implies\frac{}{P\vdash\Gamma,x{:}A_{j}}

6.

\frac{\forall i\in I.~P_{i}\vdash\Gamma,x{:}A_{i}}{x.\mathtt{case}\{i:P_{i}\}_{i\in I}\vdash\Gamma,x{:}{{\binampersand}}\{i:A_{i}\}}\implies\forall i\in I.~\frac{}{P_{i}\vdash\Gamma,x{:}A_{i}}

7.

\frac{P\vdash\Gamma,x{:}A}{\overline{x}.\mathtt{some};P\vdash\Gamma,x{:}{{\binampersand}}A}\implies\frac{}{P\vdash\Gamma,x{:}A}

8.

\frac{}{\overline{x}.\mathtt{none}\vdash x{:}{{\binampersand}}A}\implies\frac{}{\bm{0}\vdash\emptyset}

9.

\frac{P\vdash w_{1}{:}{{\binampersand}}B_{1},\ldots,w_{n}{:}{{\binampersand}}B_{n},x{:}A}{{x.\mathtt{some}}_{w_{1},\ldots,w_{n}};P\vdash w_{1}{:}{{\binampersand}}B_{1},\ldots,w_{n}{:}{{\binampersand}}B_{n},x{:}{\oplus}A}\implies\frac{}{P\vdash w_{1}{:}{{\binampersand}}B_{1},\ldots,w_{n}{:}{{\binampersand}}B_{n},x{:}A}\frac{\frac{}{\overline{w_{1}}.\mathtt{none}\vdash w_{1}{:}{{\binampersand}}B_{1}}\quad\textsc{fraction={---}}\quad\ldots\quad\frac{}{\overline{w_{n}}.\mathtt{none}\vdash w_{n}{:}{{\binampersand}}B_{n}}\quad\textsc{fraction={---}}}{\overline{w_{1}}.\mathtt{none}\mathbin{|}\ldots\mathbin{|}\overline{w_{n}}.\mathtt{none}\vdash w_{1}{:}{{\binampersand}}B_{1},\ldots,w_{n}{:}{{\binampersand}}B_{n}}\quad\textsc{,fraction={===}}

10.

This item depends on whether
x\in\mathit{fn}(P)
.

•

x\in\mathit{fn}(P)
:

\frac{\frac{P\{x^{\prime}/x\}\vdash\Gamma,y{:}A,x^{\prime}{:}{?}A}{{?}\overline{x}[y];(P\{x^{\prime}/x\})\vdash\Gamma,x{:}{?}A,x^{\prime}{:}{?}A}}{{?}\overline{x}[y];P\vdash\Gamma,x{:}{?}A}\frac{Q\vdash{?}\Delta,z{:}\overline{A}}{{!}x(z);Q\vdash{?}\Delta,x{:}{!}\overline{A}}\implies\frac{\frac{\frac{P\vdash\Gamma,y{:}A,x{:}{?}A\hskip 7.3612ptQ\{y/z\}\{w^{\prime}/w\}_{w\in{?}\Delta}\vdash{?}\Delta^{\prime},y{:}\overline{A}}{(\bm{\nu}y)(P\mathbin{|}Q\{y/z\}\{w^{\prime}/w\}_{w\in{?}\Delta})\vdash\Gamma,{?}\Delta^{\prime},x{:}{?}A}\hskip 8.19447pt{!}x(z);Q\vdash{?}\Delta,x{:}{!}\overline{A}}{(\bm{\nu}x)((\bm{\nu}y)(P\mathbin{|}Q\{y/z\}\{w^{\prime}/w\}_{w\in{?}\Delta})\mathbin{|}{!}x(z);Q)\vdash\Gamma,{?}\Delta,{?}\Delta^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{fraction={---}}}{(\bm{\nu}x)((\bm{\nu}y)(P\mathbin{|}Q\{y/z\})\mathbin{|}{!}x(z);Q)\vdash\Gamma,{?}\Delta}\quad\textsc{,fraction={===}}

•

x\notin\mathit{fn}(P)
:

\frac{P\vdash\Gamma,y{:}A}{{?}\overline{x}[y];P\vdash\Gamma,x{:}{?}A}\frac{Q\vdash{?}\Delta,z{:}\overline{A}}{{!}x(z);Q\vdash{?}\Delta,x{:}{!}\overline{A}}\implies\frac{\frac{\frac{\frac{P\vdash\Gamma,y{:}A\hskip 7.3612ptQ\{y/z\}\{w^{\prime}/w\}_{w\in{?}\Delta}\vdash{?}\Delta^{\prime},y{:}\overline{A}}{(\bm{\nu}y)(P\mathbin{|}Q\{y/z\}\{w^{\prime}/w\}_{w\in{?}\Delta})\vdash\Gamma,{?}\Delta^{\prime}}}{(\bm{\nu}y)(P\mathbin{|}Q\{y/z\}\{w^{\prime}/w\}_{w\in{?}\Delta})\vdash\Gamma,{?}\Delta^{\prime},x{:}{?}A}\hskip 8.19447pt{!}x(z);Q\vdash{?}\Delta,x{:}{!}\overline{A}}{(\bm{\nu}x)((\bm{\nu}y)(P\mathbin{|}Q\{y/z\}\{w^{\prime}/w\}_{w\in{?}\Delta})\mathbin{|}{!}x(z);Q)\vdash\Gamma,{?}\Delta,{?}\Delta^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{fraction={---}}}{(\bm{\nu}x)((\bm{\nu}y)(P\mathbin{|}Q\{y/z\})\mathbin{|}{!}x(z);Q)\vdash\Gamma,{?}\Delta}\quad\textsc{,fraction={===}}

The inductive cases follow straightforwardly.
Notice that the conditions on the bound and free names of the ND-contexts in
items

4
and
10
make sure that no names are captured when embedding one context in the other.
∎

Theorem 0.E.2

(SR for the Eager Semantics)

If
P\vdash\Gamma
and
P\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\longrightarrow}}Q
, then
Q\vdash\Gamma
.

Proof.

By induction on the derivation of the reduction.

[{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\shortrightarrow}_{\text{Id}}]

[{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.01}\shortrightarrow}_{\mathtt{some}}]
